### DESIGN AND TEST OF A LAB-SCALE N<sub>2</sub>O/HTPB HYBRID ROCKET

### Otto Krauss

AIAA Member: 217363 Aerospace Engineering Sciences Undergraduate University of Colorado at Boulder Boulder, CO

### Abstract

The MaCH-SR1 project involved the design of a 1000lb thrust hybrid rocket with the effort being undertaken during two semesters by 8 senior aerospace engineering students at the University of Colorado at Boulder. The 2002-2003 team was the second year for the project, as the previous team built two static engines and performed test burns on both. The hybrid implemented a liquid O<sub>2</sub>/HTPB oxidizer/fuel combination, but this year's team switched to a N2O/HTPB combination due to the oxidizer's simplicity and safety. Four lab-scale chambers were constructed and hot-fired in order to determine the fuel regression rate, data acquisition, and to verify the overall design for the 1000-lb thrust hybrid rocket chamber. The full-scale rocket is to be test fired in the spring of 2003 and data will be obtained for the thrust, chamber and feed pressures, and the instantaneous oxidizer mass flow rate. The lab-scale portion of the project resulted in a well-documented design and test program, which laid the foundation for future teams to build larger and more powerful engines, leading to the eventual goal of CU's first student built sounding rocket.

### Introduction

The MaCH-SR1 project aims at building a sounding rocket to deliver a 10-lb payload to the edge

of space, about 125 km above the Earth's surface. A single stage hybrid design has been chosen for the task, since it is safer than either a purely liquid or solid rocket. It is true that the hybrid has decreased performance than a liquid engine, but is less complicated and actually performs better than solid rockets. The hybrid's solid fuel will only burn in the presence of a liquid oxidizer, which can be throttled to vary thrust. A solid rocket has both the fuel and oxidizer mixed in a solid fuel grain, and once burning begins, it cannot be controlled until burnout of the engine. A liquid engine uses highly flammable liquids, which pose safety and storage problems in addition to added complexity of the feed system.

Designing a hybrid rocket is not an easy undertaking; therefore the project was divided into various subsystems, with each team concentrating on the subsystem/s of interest. The fuel team mixed and cast the fuel into the chambers; the feed system team acquired parts for the gas feed and related plumbing; the injector team designed and machined injector plates; the nozzle and chamber team optimized the nozzle for altitude and built the chambers to withstand thermal and structural stresses; the data acquisition team focused on the sensors recording data; and the system integration lead made sure all the components fit and worked together.

The full scale, 1000-lb thrust design required the construction of small, lab-scale chambers to give the group an idea of what the design process for a

hybrid engine involved, and to provide a learning curve. In the process, the regression rate for the fuel/oxidizer combination was determined, data was acquired of the chamber and feed pressures, and the effort involved in successfully testing the engines was realized.

### Design

Most of the design work for the lab-scale rocket was done in SolidWorks, a popular 3D CAD/Solid Modeling package. In addition, analysis of the chamber properties was carried out in Cosmos/Works, an add-on module that is an integrated mesher/solver/post-processing software package.

### **Performance**

The main consideration for the chamber design was thrust. Once this quantity was decided upon, other performance parameters could be determined. For the thermodynamic parameters, GDL ProPEP from the former Martin Marietta Corporation (Lockheed Martin) was used to provide initial estimates of the combustion products mass, ratio of specific heats, and specific impulse. An equation summary is presented below: Coefficient of Thrust:

$$C_F = \sqrt{k^2 \left(\frac{2}{k+1}\right)^{2k/(k-1)}} + \left(\frac{2}{k+1}\right)^{k/(k-1)} - \frac{p_a}{p_c} \tag{1}$$

Impulse: 
$$Ft_b$$
 (2)

Specific Impulse: 
$$\frac{C_F c^*}{g_0}$$
 (3)

Characteristic exhaust velocity: 
$$c^* = \frac{c}{C_F}$$
 (4)

Propellant mass flow rate: 
$$\dot{m} = \frac{p_c A_t}{c^*}$$
 (5)

Fuel mass: 
$$m_f = \frac{m_p}{MR + 1}$$
 (6)

In the above equations, k is the ratio of specific heats  $c_p/c_v$ ,  $p_a$  is atmospheric pressure while  $p_a$  is chamber pressure, and c is the effective exhaust velocity. In addition, A is the throat cross-sectional area,  $m_p$  is the total propellant mass, and MR is the oxidizer to fuel mixture ratio. All parameters were determined at a chamber pressure of 500 psi. The calculations were also performed under the assumption of frozen flow through the nozzle to approximate the low end of performance. In reality, performance is between frozen and shifting flow assumptions, since the combustion products change composition during expulsion from the nozzle, leading to slightly higher performance than frozen flow.

Knowing the fuel and oxidizer combination, a graph of the specific impulse could be made as a function of the mixture ratio. Figure 1 shows the specific impulse as a function of mixture ratio, created with GDL ProPEP.

![](_page_140_Figure_13.jpeg)

Figure 1 - Theoretical Performance

### Nozzle

The nozzle was designed for a single altitude, as the engines are being static tested and do not need to be optimized for a flight trajectory. All the testing is being conducted in Denver, Colorado, so the design atmospheric pressure is 12.1 psi. A converging nozzle implementing a straight plug section and a 45° converging section was used. The nozzle throat section was gradually rounded to provide a smooth transition for gas flow and to minimize erosive burning. Graphite was chosen as the material of choice, since it is very tolerant of high temperatures due to its low thermal expansion and high thermal conductivity, in addition to having been successfully used in past nozzles<sup>7</sup>. Graphite's ablative properties are also attractive for the application since some of the heat is taken away during operation of the chamber. A standard plug design was chosen because other similar lab-scale engines also employed this method<sup>3</sup>, and measuring thrust was not a goal for the lab-scales. To maintain chamber pressure, this design sufficed and kept the design simple. The following equations in detail the nozzle design:

Nozzle throat area: 
$$A_t = \frac{F}{C_f p_c}$$
 (7)

Nozzle exit velocity: 
$$V_e = \frac{F}{\dot{m}}$$
 (8)

where F is the thrust exerted by the chamber. The resulting converging nozzle is illustrated in Figure 2.

![](_page_141_Picture_0.jpeg)

Figure 2 - Lab-Scale Nozzle

### Fuel

Hydroxyl-Terminated Poly-Butadiene (HTPB) was the primary component of the fuel, the exact formula having been worked out by the previous year's team. Other ingredients included Isophorone Diisocyanate (IPDI), Carbon Black, and Castor Oil. The approximate component percentages are shown in Table 1.

| Table 1      |         |
|--------------|---------|
| HTPB         | 84.57%  |
| IPDI         | 10.34%  |
| Carbon Black | 0.09%   |
| Castor Oil   | 5.00%   |
| Total        | 100.00% |

The fuel port was sized by the oxidizer mass flux, with the length determined by preliminary regression rate exponents and the desired oxidizer to fuel mixture ratio. Due to the uncertainty of the exponents, a safety factor of 2 was employed for the worst-case regression rate in the design equations. This and other equations governing the fuel grain are listed below:

Fuel regression rate: 
$$\dot{r} = aG_{ox}^n$$
 (9)

Fuel port length: 
$$L = \frac{\dot{m}_f / N}{2\pi R \rho \dot{r}}$$
 (10)

The above equations show a and n as the regression rate exponents,  $G_{ox}$  as the oxidizer mass flux, R the fuel port radius,  $\rho$  as the fuel density, and N as the number of fuel ports. Due to simplicity and available tubing sizes, the fuel port diameter was chosen as 1.01 inches, with the port length being about 9.25 inches. The remaining parameters were determined accordingly.

### Combustion Chamber

The combustion chamber was designed to be a pressure vessel capable of withstanding the thermal and dynamic stresses placed upon it during firing. Due to the unknown nature of this particular hybrid, stainless steel 304 tubing was used for the lab-scale chambers, with a .125-inch wall. The length of fuel cast inside,

plus a 0.5 length to diameter ratio for both the pre and post combustor sections, determined the chamber's length. The fuel is designed to act as insulation during each burn due to its material properties. However, the pre and post combustors were both exposed to the steel of the chamber. Therefore, phenolic spacers were designed to form an ablative barrier between the wall and the hot gases undergoing combustion. Glass phenolic has a very low thermal conductivity, and is therefore well suited for insulation. The lab-scale chamber diameter was chosen to provide a significant factor of safety for short-duration burns and to also minimize the cost of components. To calculate the failure modes and safety factors of the chambers, the following equations<sup>2</sup> were employed:

Tangential Stress: 
$$\sigma_t = \frac{r_i^2 p_i}{r_0^2 - r_i^2} \left( 1 + \frac{r_0^2}{r^2} \right)$$
 (11)

Radial Stress: 
$$\sigma_t = \frac{r_i^2 p_i}{r_0^2 - r_i^2} \left( 1 - \frac{r_0^2}{r^2} \right)$$
 (12)

Allowable working pressure: 
$$p = \frac{2t\sigma_{t \max}}{d_i + t}$$
 (13)

Bolt Stress: 
$$\sigma_{bolt} = \frac{F_b}{A_t}$$
 (14)

The calculations showed that even with a 1000-psi pressure spike in the chamber, a reasonable factor of safety is maintained. Pressure spikes are an important consideration, since a badly designed rocket will suffer from combustion instabilities, resulting in pressure spikes and possible failure. Table 2 lists the failure scenarios of the lab-scale chamber.

 Table 2

 Failure
 P. (psi)

 Tangential
 3328

 Radial
 40000

 Bolt
 44763

 Seal
 4732

A thermal analysis was carried out of the lab-scale assembly assuming the fuel port burn area reached halfway to the chamber, and the thickness was found to be adequate to contain combustion. An area of concern however was the nozzle, but with the addition of EPDM insulation, the system was capable of being safely operated.

### Ignition

The ignition of the rocket chambers was decided upon after doing research on other similar hybrids<sup>4, 8, 9</sup>, and which method was the most reliable and simplest. The idea of preheating HTPB by applying

current to steel wool inside the fuel port seemed like an attractive option. Electrical leads were loosely wrapped with steel wool, which was then placed snugly up the fuel port. The leads were then connected to a car battery. The pre-combustion chamber was then filled up with gaseous oxygen for several seconds to ensure an oxygen-rich environment. Then current was applied across the leads, causing the wool to burn and heating the surrounding HTPB fuel sufficiently for combustion to occur. In all, four tests were conducted to validate this ignition method, with success in everytrial.

### Iniector

The injector plate was designed to provide the desired flow rates into the thrust chamber at the same time turning the liquid oxidizer into gas (atomization). For this purpose, the injector holes were ordered in a radial showerhead pattern to distribute the flow evenly across the fuel port. Separate holes were added to accommodate gaseous oxygen feeds and pressure transducers. A cavity was designed in the center of the plate to provide a seat for a pipe nipple, which would hook to the feed system. The following equations drove the design:

Mass flow rate: 
$$\dot{m} = C_d A_i \sqrt{2\rho\Delta p}$$
 (15)

Oxidizer injection velocity: 
$$V_i = C_d \sqrt{\frac{2\Delta p}{\rho}}$$
 (16)

Where  $\rho$  is the oxidizer density,  $\Delta p$  is the pressure drop,  $A_i$  is the total injection hole cross-sectional area, and  $C_d$  is the discharge coefficient. Injector test pieces were tested with water to determine the mass flow rate and the resulting pressure drop. Two lab-scale injectors were designed, for 0.15 kg/s and 0.25 kg/s. The following diagram in Figure 3 illustrates the injector pattern with varying hole sizes that was tested.

![](_page_142_Picture_6.jpeg)

Figure 3 - Lab-Scale Injector Pattern

The tests were carried out after attaching an injector piece to a pressurized tank assembly, which also had a gauge to measure the difference between the tank and atmospheric pressures. See Figure 4 for the injector test.

![](_page_142_Picture_9.jpeg)

Figure 4 – Injector Hydraulic Testing

Figure 5.a shows the mass flow rate as a function of the pressure drop. The mass flow follows an almost linear behavior, allowing for extrapolation of pressure drops for higher mass flows. Figure 5.b shows the Discharge Coefficient versus pressure drop. Note the non-linearity of the C<sub>d</sub> as the pressure drop hits 60 psi. This is a result of using two different pressure gages, one relatively accurate to 60 psi, and the other less accurate to 100 psi. This is a possible source of error for the pressure drop prediction at 0.15 and 0.25 kg/s of expected flow rate. However, the data was extrapolated to a predicted drop of 300 psi for 0.15 kg/s and 180 psi for 0.25-kg/s. Due to the measurement of Cd using two gages, no conclusive C<sub>d</sub> value could be determined, and as a result a value of 0.65 was taken from Sutton<sup>1</sup> for an orifice size of ~1mm (0.025 in). A second injector design had a different hole size of 0.032 inches, but hydraulic testing was not performed due to time constraints.

![](_page_142_Figure_12.jpeg)

Figure 5.a - Mass Flow Rate

![](_page_143_Figure_0.jpeg)

Figure 5.b - Discharge Coefficient

The lab-scale injector is shown below in Figure 6 and two holes are clearly seen adjacent to the main injection holes. The first hole was used for the injection of gaseous oxygen to facilitate ignition of the fuel, and the second hole was for the pressure transducer fitting.

![](_page_143_Picture_3.jpeg)

Figure 6 - Lab-Scale Injector Plate

The injector pattern itself was decided to be a simple radial showerhead design with 12 holes of similar size spread out on two bolt circles with diameters of 0.2 and 0.4 inches, adequate for clearance on a standard 1–inch pipe nipple, and coverage of the fuel port as well.

### Feed System

The feed system for the static firings was composed of a series of valves, pressure regulators, transducers, check valves, and stainless steel tubing. The feed system provided oxygen for the ignition, oxidizer for the thrust chamber during firing, and Nitrogen to purge the system after each test. The main concern was the pressure drop across the valves and the injector. To design for the chamber pressure, the properties of Nitrous Oxide (N<sub>2</sub>O) were examined at the expected temperatures of testing. The graph in Figure 7

shows the liquid density of  $N_2O$  at a range of temperatures.

![](_page_143_Figure_9.jpeg)

Figure 7 - Properties of N2O

Also, Figure 8.a illustrates the feed system diagram with all associated plumbing and a legend explaining the symbols.

![](_page_143_Figure_12.jpeg)

Figure 8.a - Feed system Schematic

![](_page_143_Picture_14.jpeg)

Figure 8.b - Feed System Solid Model w/o Tanks

Figure 8.b is a solid model of the chamber hooked up to the feed system tubing without tanks attached.

### Data Acquisition

The sensors involved in taking measurements for the lab scale engines were two pressure transducers, one measuring the feed pressure, and the other reading the chamber pressure. In addition to the real time pressure readings, the oxidizer tanks were weighed prior to and after each firing, so the average mass flow rate could be measured. The primary data acquisition system consisted of a Hewlett Packard OmniBook laptop computer with a data acquisition card, connected to an SC-2345 Signal Conditioner. Eight channels were available for use, with the 2 pressure transducers providing real-time data. See Figure 9 for a schematic.

![](_page_144_Figure_3.jpeg)

Figure 9 - Data Acquisition System for Testing

Voltage output from the transducers was fed into LabView, where the voltages are calibrated to correspond to different pressures. The data was then imported into an Excel spreadsheet for analysis.

### Test Stand

The design for the lab-scale test stand was basically driven by the need to just clamp the chamber down during firing, preventing any movement. Originally, the design called for a uni-strut tube configuration<sup>6</sup>, with the chamber bolted on with tube fasteners. To interface between the chamber and the test stand, a metal plate was designed to not only adhere to the contour of the chamber bottom in a horizontal fashion, but to also bolt down onto the top of the test stand. See picture in Figure 10 for initial design.

![](_page_144_Picture_8.jpeg)

Figure 10 - Initial Test Stand Design

Figure 11 shows the culmination of the design effort in a lab-scale hybrid rocket chamber. The cross-sectional view allows all parts of the system to be seen.

![](_page_144_Picture_11.jpeg)

Figure 11 - Final Chamber Design

### Construction

### Nozzle

The nozzle was machined out of medium grade graphite, with relatively large grain size. A single 3-inch rod of graphite was cut into four pieces, representing each lab-scale nozzle. After mounting on the lathe, each piece was faced off and the inner contour was milled out. Using heavy grit sandpaper, the throat was rounded. Then a splitter was used to cut the finished nozzle from the graphite stock. Since graphite is a hazardous material to work with, respirators had to be worn during machining. See Figure 12.

![](_page_144_Picture_16.jpeg)

Figure 12 - Nozzle Machining

### Fuel

Preliminary testing of the fuel mixtures in small amounts was done to determine the best mandrill release agent. After several variations, it was determined that saran wrap was the best medium between the fuel and the mandrill. The casting involved the construction of fuel plugs, which provided the sealing of the chamber bottom and insured the mandrill

stayed in place. Two lab-scale engines were cured at the same time, after fuel was poured into each and the distance from the fuel top to the chamber top was measured. The first batch of two chambers unfortunately contained the wrong mixture ratios of the fuel and had to be scrubbed out using Methyl Ethyl Ketone (MEK). The next batches were successful, curing in the ovens for about 4 days at 165° F. It was discovered at the testing site however, that the fuel in the chambers cracked longitudinally along the fuel port, due to the high temperatures during curing, which placed considerable stress into the fuel. It was decided that for the full scale, the curing temperatures would be around 135° F for a week or more. Primary obstacles included casting the fuel without including air bubbles in the mixture and finding a suitable means of keeping the fuel surface smooth during curing. The saran wrap had a slight tendency to wrinkle, no matter how tightly it was wrapped around the mandrills. Due to the high bonding strength of the fuel, the mandrills had to be taken out using an industrial press, and the fuel port was sufficiently smooth and round.

### Combustion Chamber

The chambers for the lab-scale engines were built by purchasing 304 stainless steel tubing, which was cut into sections to provide 4 engines. The chambers had Con-Flanges welded onto the ends, allowing for a seal interface for the injector on one end, and the nozzle on the other. The welding was done on a turntable using a TIG welder. The flanges were eyeballed to make sure they were perpendicular to the tube. First they were spot welded, then turned upright, so the full welding between the Con-flange and the steel tube could be finished. Pressure testing was performed on one chamber, using a hydraulic pump to provide the pressure. Pressures of 1500 psi were reached, validating the safety of the welding technique and providing for an operating pressure safety factor of 3 (based on a 500-psi chamber pressure).

### Injector

The injector plates were machined out of solid Con-Flat blanks, eliminating a significant amount of machining time, since the boltholes were already in place. First, a small seat for the nipple hookup to the feed system was machined into the center of the injector. For the lab-scale, the injector holes were very small (0.025 and 0.032 in diameter), requiring EDM (Electron Discharge Machining) to be implemented. Sealing of the injector plates to the chamber was accomplished using copper gaskets crushed between Con-Flat flanges. The gaskets placed provided a metalmetal seal, ensuring a leak free system.

### Ignition

The ignition system required minimal construction, as the main components were readily available. This included a car battery, some electrical leads, and steel wool, which were sold in packages available at the local hardware store.

### Feed System

The feed system involved the purchase of numerous fittings. The lab-scale involved 0.25 inch tubing for the Nitrous Oxide since the flow rates were not significant. The gaseous Oxygen and Nitrogen used 0.125 tubing and were connected in the system as shown in Figure 8.a. All the gases were bought from a local supplier and the necessary safety precautions were observed in addition to completion of the needed paperwork with the University of Colorado. Solenoid valves from the previous year's team were used, and regulators were placed onto the Nitrogen, Helium, and gaseous Oxygen feeds. The N<sub>2</sub>O flow was regulated with a Helium head pressurized to 1400 psi, and thus, no regulator was needed for the oxidizer. See Figure 13 for the tank setup.

![](_page_145_Picture_9.jpeg)

Figure  $13 - N_2$ ,  $O_2$ , and  $N_2O$  tanks

### Test Stand

The test stand was a store bought half-shelf unit or (Gorilla Rack) that provided adequate support. The wooden planks were screwed into the metal girders, and the girders were welded together at each joint. The metal interface plate was squared on the mill, and the CNC mill then cut out the contour on the aluminum plate. See Figure 14 for a view of the test stand with one of the hybrid rocket chambers attached.

![](_page_146_Picture_0.jpeg)

Figure 14 - Test Stand with Chamber Attached

### Testing

Testing of the lab-scale rocket chambers took place at Pioneer Astronautics in Lakewood, Colorado during the 2002/03 winter break. The test coordinators at the facility were Mark Caviezel and Gary Snyder. Their facility had a test cell that has been previously used for other rocket engine firings, and the unique concrete-bunker style of the cell was adequate for the lab-scale firings. See Figure 15.

![](_page_146_Picture_4.jpeg)

Figure 15 – Pioneer Astronautics Test Cell

Mission Control was set up inside the Pioneer Astronautics building with a window providing a link to the test cell by accommodating the wiring and other cables for the various electronics. Two video cameras were used; one recording the test fires (Chamber Cam), and the other was focused on the regulator and pressure gauge (Gauge Cam) in the feed system to verify transducer measurements. The pressure transducer readings were initially set for a 1000 samples/second, but for the later firings, a sampling rate of 250 samples/second was deemed adequate due to excessive data points. See Figure 16 for pictures of Mission Control and the Chamber Setup in the test cell.

![](_page_146_Picture_7.jpeg)

Figure 16.a – Mission Control

![](_page_146_Picture_9.jpeg)

Figure 16.b - Camera setup

Four Lab-Scale chambers were tested and video of each firing along with pictures of the final two burns were obtained. See Figure 17.

![](_page_146_Picture_12.jpeg)

![](_page_146_Picture_13.jpeg)

Figure 17 - Firings No. 3 & 4

The equipment setup and testing was completed over a period of 5 days in late December and early January, during which time all four chambers were tested, with three providing sufficient data to move onto the full-scale design. The team was satisfied with the progress made in the fall semester that led to the successful testing of the hybrid rocket chambers and the team took a moment to pose for a group picture in Figure 18.

![](_page_147_Picture_0.jpeg)

Figure 18 – The MaCH-SRI Team L to R: Xalin Horne, Nick Patzer, Erica Raine, Otto Krauss, Tala Mastalir, and Mike Simpson. (Not pictured: Tom White and Dustin McCarthy)

### Results

The objective of the testing was to fire four identical chambers, but with two different mass flow rates and two different burn times. This would allow the solving of simultaneous regression rate equations, thus determining the regression rate exponents, a and n.

Initial difficulty with the electrical system, the ignition system, and the N2O solenoid valve delayed testing. The issue with the ignition involved the steel wool having been too densely packed around the electrical leads, thus when current was applied, the spark was spread out over a much larger surface area. This problem was resolved by hooking up the leads to a 120-Volt Variac, which provided instantaneous spark capability, as opposed to the 12-Volt car battery tried earlier. The problem with the solenoid valve initially was that it would freeze open after actuation. It wasn't discovered until later that the solenoid used DC current and the switch in the control box was AC. As a result, the first chamber firing lasted longer than the planned 5 seconds, burning fuel for a full 27 seconds before reaching the chamber wall at the nozzle end, and then burning for another 21 seconds a mixture of stainless steel and residual fuel upstream of the nozzle. After depletion of the fuel, the oxidizer tank was manually turned off and Nitrogen purged the system for about a minute. Upon inspection, the chamber was thoroughly cleaned of fuel and the worst-case scenario was illustrated without any chamber failure, to the relief of the team. Unfortunately, the pressure readings were abruptly stopped 8 seconds after ignition due to confusion among the team as to why the chamber didn't stop firing after the planned 5 seconds. In addition, the Chamber Cam was wrapped with bubble wrap to provide stability on a slightly damaged tripod, and during the first burn the bubble wrap melted, causing the camera to tilt down after about 15 seconds. The

Gauge Cam however was used to determine the total time the system was pressurized, thus allowing for an approximate determination of the burn time and the feed and chamber pressures as well. Disconnecting the solenoid wire from the switch and manually putting it on a car battery terminal resolved the valve problem. The remaining three tests were carried out without any problems. A summary of the test results versus what was planned is presented in Table 3.

Table 3 – Test Summary

| Chamber                   | D      | С    | В    | Α    |
|---------------------------|--------|------|------|------|
|                           | Planne | d    |      |      |

| Oxidizer Flow Rate (kg/s) | 0.15   | 0.15 | 0.25 | 0.25 |
| Average Mixture Ratio     | 6      | 6    | 6    | 6    |
| Average Chamber P. (psi)  | 500    | 500  | 500  | 500  |
|                           | Actual |      |      |      |
| Burn Time (s)             | 27     | 4.75 | 4.6  | 10.5 |
| Oxidizer Flow Rate (kg/s) | 0.15   | 0.21 | 0.17 | 0.16 |
| Average Mixture Ratio     | 5.7    | 4.6  | 4.8  | 5.2  |
| Average Chamber P. (psi)  | 193    | 280  | 280  | 253  |

Even though the results for the burn times and mass flow rates were off from design values, the average regression rate for each chamber was still determined. Since the first burn (Chamber D) was unreliable for various reasons, corresponding data was not used in the fuel regression analysis. See Table 4 for a summary and Figure 19 for the observed regression curve.

Table 4 - Regression Results

| İ | Chamber | r-dot(in/s) | L(in) | G₀x(lbm/s-in²) | G     |
|---|---------|-------------|-------|----------------|-------|
|   | С       | 0.0675      | 9.174 | 0.578          | 0.679 |
|   | В       | 0.0548      | 9.325 | 0.468          | 0.55  |
|   | Α       | 0.0406      | 9.475 | 0.44           | 0.517 |

![](_page_147_Figure_11.jpeg)

Figure 19 – HTPB Regression Curve

The regression curve in Figure 19 illustrates that as the oxidizer mass flux increased along the fuel port, so did the average regression rate of the fuel. This was as expected, based on other hybrid results presented in

Sutton and Larson. Putting the results of Table 4 into matrix form and solving for a 1000-lb thrust, 500-psi chamber pressure hybrid design, the following parameters were determined in Table 5.

Table 5 – Full-Scale Hybrid Regression Parameters

a 0.1160
N 0.9874

Table 6 shows that the maximum chamber pressures attained in each firing did not exceed about 300 psi, not quite the 500 psi originally designed for.

Table 6 - Tank Pressures

| 24010                                             |             |             |             |             |
|---------------------------------------------------|-------------|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| Chamber                                           | D           | С           | В           | Α           |
| Max Tank Press. (psi)<br>Max Chamber Press. (psi) | 1000<br>220 | 1400<br>297 | 1400<br>305 | 1400<br>312 |
| Max Feed Sys. Press.(psi)                         |             | 1131        | <b>7</b> 81 | 792         |

The following graphs in Figure 20 present the feed system and chamber pressure graphs as recorded by the pressure transducers on the system. Each graph is presented in the order that it was recorded.

![](_page_148_Figure_6.jpeg)

Figure 20.a - Chamber D Pressure Results

The data is cut off for the first chamber firing as mentioned earlier, but the extrapolated data lines based on the Gauge Cam overlap the transducer data and complete the data set. The chamber pressure was seen as peaking around 774 psi, which was lower than the 1000 psi in the oxidizer tank. This was the result of a more than 200-psi pressure drop through the solenoid valves and tubing leading up to the transducer in the feed system. The CGA-326 fitting on the N2O bottle is also partly responsible for the drop, since the orifice size on the fitting is only 0.096 inches in diameter. The pressure on the chamber transducer recorded a peak value of 220-psi, indicating a large loss through the injector. This was because the injector inlet may already have seen gas due to the expansion of the oxidizer from 0.25 inch tubing to a 1-inch diameter by 4-inch long pipe section. This was unanticipated and will be taken into account for the full-scale design. This loss is on top of the restricting orifices of the injectors contributing another ~300-psi in losses. The 0.032-inch orifices were not as restrictive as the highest chamber pressures were reached with that injector hole size.

![](_page_148_Figure_10.jpeg)

Figure 20.b - Chamber C Pressure Results

The main difference in firings 3 & 4 from the first 2 is that it took several seconds for the chamber pressure to build up in the pre-combustor. This has been theorized as due to the larger holes providing less of a resistance to flow, therefore allowing the ullage volume in the nipple to be pressurized at a slower rate.

![](_page_148_Figure_13.jpeg)

Figure 20.c - Chamber B Pressure Results

![](_page_148_Figure_15.jpeg)

Figure 20.d - Chamber A Pressure Results

An initial concern the team had was combustion instability, as some hybrids have experienced this in past experiments. But according to the graphs in Figure 20, the pressure profiles are relatively smooth curves, with no adverse pressure spikes present. Also note in the graphs that as the burn progresses, the pressures decay gradually in the feed system. This is due to the fact that the Helium head on the N<sub>2</sub>O produced essentially a blow-down system that will naturally decay as gas expelled over time. The initial plan called for a regulator on the N<sub>2</sub>O tank, but one couldn't be obtained for the needed pressure and flow rates to be experienced during testing.

### Conclusion

The fall semester of ASEN 4018: Senior Design Projects came to a successful conclusion for the MaCH-SR1 team and their lab-scale hybrid rocket effort. The complete engineering process was carried out in the semester, starting with research and moving through design, construction, and testing of actual hardware. The main goal of characterizing the oxidizer/fuel combination was achieved, in addition to exposing the group to all aspects of a team-oriented project. A lesson of primary importance was the integration of various subsystems and how they interface with each other. Not everything went according to plan, but now a more thorough understanding of a complex system such as a rocket propulsion system has been gained. The completion of this phase of the project allowed the team to go ahead with the full-scale 1000-lb thrust hybrid engine with experience already gained for the task.

### 로켓의 열전달 이슈 소개

- The Issues of Heat Transfter for Rockets -

담당 : 구자예교수님

항공우주공학전공4

학번 : 2000121134

성명 : 윤 창 진

### 1. SRM의 열전달 이슈

내면연소의 경우, 고체추진제가 연소가스로부터 연소실 벽면을 보호하는 이점이 있으나, 노즐 벽면은 고온 연소가스에 노출된다. 이 경우의 냉각에서는 액체냉각제가 없기 때문에 삭마(용융) 냉각, heat sink 등의 방법을 사용한다.

### - 최근 국내 연구 분야

2상 유동장 해석

TVC용 let Vane 주위 유동 및 열설계 기술 등

### 2. LRE의 열전달 이슈

연소실 및 노즐의 전체 벽면이 고온의 연소 가스에 의해 노출되지만 액상 냉각제를 사용하여 적극적인 재생냉각, 필름 냉각이 가능하다.

### - 최근 국내 연구 분야

재생냉각 기술

막냉각 기술

내열재 설계 기술

가스발생기의 열교환기 설계 기술, 열전달 해석 기술

극저온/열/고압 응력 및 변형예측 기술

터보펌프내 극저온 흡입성능 예측/시험 기술 등

### 3. 엔진 내부 열전달

### 1) 연소가스-벽면 간의 대류 열전달

로켓 엔진의 연소실 내 유동이 강한 난류 상태임은 쉽게 예상할 수 있다. 액체 로켓의 경우 분사기에 가까운 영역에서는 특히 추진제 농도 또는 가스 온도의 변동도 급격하게 된다고 생각할 수 있다. 분사판 표면 또는 그에 근접한 연소실측 벽면에 대한 열전달은 각각의 분사 요소에 관련된 국부적인 조건에 따르며, 이 부분에서의 벽면 파손은 단순히 열적인 것뿐만이 아니라, 추진제의 부식성, 산화성의 영향도 큰 것에 주의할 필요가 있다. 고속유동을 고려한 노즐 벽면의 열전달은 그 해석에 있어서, 단면적이 일정하지 않고, 완전히 발달된 관내 유동이라는 조건도 성립되지 않아 어려움이 있다.

### 2) 연소실 내의 복사열전달

### - 가스 열복사

전자파의 파장이 적외선 영역 $(0.8 \, \mu m < \lambda < 1 mm)$ 에 존재하는 복사를 열복사라 하는데 연소 가스에 다량으로 존재하는  $CO_2$ ,  $H_2O$ 에 의해 방출된다.

### - 화학발광에 관련된 복사

화학반응대에 일시적으로 생성된 많은 중간 생성물이 자외선 영역에 걸쳐 스펙트럼을 내며, 이를 화학 발광이라 하며, 실제 영향이 매우 적으므로, 열전달 측면에서 문제가 되지는 않는다.

### - 고체입자의 열복사

연소가스 중에 soot 및 산화물 입자가 존재할 경우, 이 고체입자는 연속스펙트럼을 갖는 복사를 발생하고, 연소가스는 황적색으로 빛나게 된다. 이러한 화염을 휘염이라 부르며, 비휘염에 비해 강한 열복사를 낸다.

### 3) 냉각용 유체 측의 열전달

- 추력실 벽을 냉각시키기 위해 벽에 유로(channel)을 설치하여 냉각용 유체를 흐르게 한다. 관로에 유입되는 유체는 보통 액체 상태이며, 냉각 유로에서는 고온 가스측으로부터 열부하가 변화하며, 보통 노즐목 부분에서 최대가 된다. 일반적으로 유동이 충류일 경우에는 열전달률이 작기 때문에 냉각용 유동은 난류화할 필요가 있다.

### 4) 벽면 냉각법

- 로켓엔진의 연소가스 온도는 2000~3500 ℃ 정도로 대단히 높으며(내열재료의 융점보다 높은 경우도 있음), 또한 열유속도 크기 때문에, 연소가스에 접촉하는 연소실 벽면 및 노즐 벽면에는 적당한 냉각이 가해져야 한다. 작동시간이 비교적 짧은 로켓에서는 연소가스에 접촉하는 추력실 벽면을 냉각하지 않고, 벽면구조를 heat sink로 작용시켜 작동 종료시까지 그 온도가 열파괴 한계내로 억제되도록 설계할 수 있다. 이 때 벽면구조는 열용량이 크고, 열전도특성이 좋은 것이어야 한다.
- 재생냉각 : 액체로켓엔진에서 광범위하게 사용됨. 연소실에 분사하기 전의 추진제를 추력실 벽 속의 관로를 통해 흘려 벽면을 냉각하는 방법이다. 연소가스로부터 벽면을 통해 유체에 유입된 열은 결국 연소가스의 엔탈피에 포함되어 효과적으로 사용되기 때문에 재생이라는 표현을 쓴다.
- 덤프냉각: 냉각제를 추력실 벽속의 관로를 통해 흘려 냉각한다는 점에서 재생 냉각과 같으나, 여기서는 사용한 액체를 연소시키지 않고 방출한다. 적당한 노즐을 사용하여 기화된 냉각제를 초음속으로 방출하면 약간의 추력을 얻을 수 있다. 이 방법은 재생냉각에 비해 성능이 약간 저하되나 구조적으로 간단하며, 또한 냉각제의 공급압력이 작아도 된다는 장점이 있다.
- 막냉각 : film cooling은 고온가스에 접촉되는 벽면을 따라 가스 혹은 액체의 냉각제를 흘려, 벽면으로의 열전달을 막는 방법이다. 액체로켓에서 냉각제로서 추진제의 하나인 연료를 사용하면 화면 근방이 과농혼합가스가 되어 연소가스온도가 저하되며 벽면으로의 열전달양이 감소한다.
- 삭마냉각 : 추력실의 연소가스측의 벽면에 삭마재료가 설치되어 이것이 용융, 증발하여 열을 흡수함과 동시에 발생한 가스가 저온의 경계층을 만들어 구조재료에 대한 과대한 열전달을 억제한다. 이런 종류의 냉각법이 유효한 시간은 삭마재료의 두께에 따라 다르며, 로켓 엔진에 대해서는 초단위 수분에 걸쳐 사용되고 있다. 이 방법은 고체 로켓에서 많이 사용되고 있으며 비교적 연소압이 낮은 가압식 액체로켓엔진에도 이용되고 있다.

- 차열라이너에 의해 열차폐 : 고온 연소가스에 노출되는 벽면에 차열라이너를 장착시킴으로써 벽면으로의 열전달 양을 감소시켜 그 온도를 낮게 유지할 수 있다.

### 5) 복사냉각

- 가열되어 고온이 된 추력실 외면에서 열을 복사시켜 냉각이 이루어진다.

### 3. 적용대상

### 1) $LH_2$ -LOX

로켓 엔진의 비추력은 배출가스의 분자량에 의존하는데 수소는 가장 작은 분자량의 원소이므로 로켓 엔진의 연료로써 가장 적합한 연료이다. 하지만  $LH_2$ 는 기화 온도(끓는점)가 -422.9°F이므로 극저온 상태에다가 가볍고, 물의 1/12정도의 밀도를 가진다. 이는탱크가 거대하고, 반드시 밀폐되어야 함을 의미한다.  $LH_2$ 가 지나는 관에 접촉하는 공기와 큰 열전달을 일으키므로, 모든 것이 밀폐되어야 한다. 우주에서 이러한 점이 문제는아니지만, 이것이 좋은 부스터의 연료로 쓰이지 못하게 한다. 비추력은 약  $360\sim500$  정도이다.

### 2) RP1-LOX

RP1의 초기 아이디어는 군용 제트 추진 원료로부터 나왔다. 정교한 로켓 엔진에 들어가기 위해서는 보다 정제된 연료가 필요하기 때문에 개발된 연료이다. V2의 미국 버전인 Redstone과 Atlas, Thor, Titan I, Saturn의 부스터들이 이를 이용하였다. 비추력은 200~300 정도이다.

# C. Interface Requirements

These are mostly design constraints including, in particular, the maximum length and diameters, launch hook features, etc. These requirements must result in the determination of the borders between the subassemblies of the missile. Table 1 gives a description of the main types of specifications for a missile and how they induce the specifications of the different subsystems.

# D. Description of a Typical Solid Propellant Rocket Motor

A solid propellant rocket motor (Fig. 1) is designed to ensure the combustion under pressure of the propellant grain it contains. The resulting gases are expanded through the nozzle whose function is to convert this internal pressure into a supersonic exhaust velocity.

As a rule, such a rocket motor has the following five major components.

1) The typical solid rocket motor case, the first component, is basically a double-domed right circular cylinder with openings at both ends and cylindrical extensions called skirts. The aft opening interfaces with the nozzle, and the forward opening accommodates the safe and arm device as well as the igniter. Additionally, numerous conduits, ducts, fin attachments, and carry features may be required on the case.

![](_page_159_Picture_13.jpeg)

Fig. 1 Solid rocket motor for tactical missile.

The solid rocket motor case is essentially a minimum-weight pressure vessel whose design, as we have seen, is complicated by the presence of significant levels of thrust and bending and locally concentrated loads from the skirts and attachments. Tactical motors typically operate from 3 to 25 MPa. Aerodynamic heating profiles may also have to be considered since temperatures as high as 800 K are possible in some cases.

2) The propellant grain is the next component. Grains are made of solid propellant shaped according to a given geometry during manufacture. To prevent combustion, their surface may be locally restricted or may be inhibited with a flame resistant adhesive material, an inhibitor. The regression of the free propellant surface, once it has begun to burn, is normal to this surface and dictates the evolution of the pressure and thrust. When the propellant grain is case bonded, a chemical bonding system, generally a liner, attaches the grain to the case wall interior and insulator.

The burning time of the propellant in tactical systems will generally vary between a few seconds and 30 s. Using end-burning grains, sustainers with a few minutes burning times are used for some long-range applications.

3) The thermal insulation is the next component. The motor case interior is covered with an insulator which provides thermal protection and flow ergion resistance to gases whose temperature ranges from 1500 K to more than 3500 K, in regions where propellant burns to the wall before the entire grain is consumed.

4) The general shape of the nozzle, the fourth component, includes three major parts: the convergent zone, which channels the gas flow; the throat, whose dimensions determine the operating pressure of the rocket motor; and the exit cone, which increases the exhaust velocity of the gases in their expansion phase, consequently improving the propulsive effect.

In tactical systems, the nozzle is sometimes placed at the end of a tube, called a blast tube, in order to provide space for different devices such as those that activate the steering controls of the missile.

 The last component, the ignition system, brings the energy necessary to the surface of the propellant to start burning.

In the case of the preliminary design of tactical motors semiempirical approaches are often applied. They use the data obtained through firing of similar motors or ballistic evaluation motors of approximately the same size loaded with the same propellants and the equations for variations of  $C_D$  and  $C_F$  previously presented.

## F. Structural Performance

The equation for the velocity increase shows that the two main parameters that characterize the performance of a motor are the specific impulse and the mass fraction, i.e., the ratio of propellant mass to inert mass (case, nozzle, insulation, igniter). From a structural point of view the coefficient often used is the ratio of the propellant mass to the total mass of the motor; Fig. 3 gives an idea of the evolution of this ratio with history for tactical motors having approximately the same size. The best performance is, of course, obtained by the use of high-specific-strength composite materials for the case and a minimum-weight design for the nozzle.

The higher the operating pressure, the higher is the specific impulse of the motor, but also the higher is the thickness and mass of the case. There must be a tradeoff and optimization of the motor design. In many cases for integral motors with high length to diameter ratios, the most important design criteria will be bending stiffness after combustion of the propellant.

![](_page_161_Figure_19.jpeg)

Fig. 3 Structural ratio evolution.

# G. General Method for the Design and Optimization of a Solid Rocket Motor

The general procedure summarized next is a typical procedure applied to a typical tactical motor. There is, of course, no such thing as a typical rocket motor, especially in the field of tactical systems, because of the great variety of designs (the range of combustion times can vary, for instance, from a few milliseconds to a few minutes) and the great variety of missions. In addition there are preferences of the company or organization developing the propulsion unit based on past experience or specialization in some types of materials for cases, nozzles, propellants, etc. Therefore, the development must be considered as a didactic description that should be amended in every specific case.

As already stated the solid rocket motor case is essentially a minimum-weight pressure vessel on which bending and locally concentrated mechanical loads are applied, also sometimes local deformations have to be considered. The design of the motor must take into account these loads from the beginning.

The preliminary design will generally start with some general technology decisions, such as type of case (composite, metallic, etc.), type of propellant (ability to case bond or not, signature characteristics, hazard classification, etc.), type of thrust vector control if any, characteristics related to vulnerability to be incorporated, characteristics related to production (for instance, type of assembly of the aft closure) and maintenance, and characteristics related to cost, etc.

The overall envelope (maximum diameter and length) is already dictated by the missile designer. The initial design will generally begin by the choice of an operating pressure (based on past experience, extreme operating temperatures, rough optimization between impulse and weight, specific considerations, etc.), and a first calculation of the case will be done with a thickness of the case material adapted to the internal pressure and to the bending moments or local deformations according to a given coefficient of safety. Coefficients of safety are often supplied to the designer and may vary from customer to customer and depend on the application. They will generally be higher for air-launch applications or man portable systems. The range is generally from 1.25 to 1.6. Local reinforcements can be represented by equivalent thicknesses. If significant aeroheating takes place the design must be checked according to the case material properties at the corresponding temperature.

At this stage analytical calculations or simple codes may be used. The result will be a first drawing of the case with a determination of the deformation properties under pressure.

The second step will be the choice of a propellant and a grain geometry able to satisfy the propulsion requirements. The choice of the main parameters of the nozzle (throat diameter and expansion ratio) is done at the same time. This preliminary design of the propellant grain can be made with the help of computer codes able to make an optimization through mechanical and ballistics calculations according to the main propulsion requirements. The margin of safety level for the operational thermal cycles taken for the propellant is an important parameter at this point. The internal thermal insulation will be then defined as a consequence of the grain geometry. The results will be thrust and pressure performance as close as possible to the requirements. These results and, especially, the value of the maximum operating pressure; the mass, and the total impulse obtained may lead to iterations on the design by modifications of the case followed by a new

adaptation of the propellant grain and insulation designs or even to a modification of the case volume or type of propellant.

The next step will generally be a review of the various design solutions and their compliance to the requirements with the missile designer. Also, production costs and capability of performance improvements will be compared. At this stage simulations of the missile performance with the various possible solutions will be performed. Functional and value analysis are efficient tools to help the program managers to make the right decisions.

When an agreement is reached a more detailed design will be undertaken. It involves more detailed calculations of the case and propellant grain, optimization of the nozzle, introduction of a possible throat erosion law for the nozzle, etc. Pressure and thrust laws at ambient and extreme temperatures are determined, and structural integrity safety margins for the various thermomechanical or aerodynamic loads are checked.

Two-dimensional codes, and today more and more three-dimensional codes, may be used. The results of these analyses may lead to the definition of future testing to be performed for some critical loads.

# III. Propellant Properties and Selection

# . Case-Bonded Grains and Free-Standing Grains

There are two main types of grain architecture: free-standing grains and case-bonded-grains. Grains of the first type are introduced into rocket motor cases (cartridge loaded) after manufacture. Grains of the second type are bonded to the motor case during the casting (or injection) and curing steps of the propellant grain manufacturing process (Fig. 4).

There is not a single, well-defined procedure for selecting a free-standing grain architecture or a case-bonded grain architecture for a given rocket motor, except when one of these two architectures is obviously most appropriate for a specific reason. Nevertheless, case-bonded grains generally give higher performances than free-standing grains for equal available volumes (the volume occupied by the inhibitor, the case insulation, and the space between them can be filled with propellant). On the other hand, the mechanical loads will be very different in the two cases.

The service life of a propellant grain before firing consists in a succession of transport and storage phases, under conditions that are contingent to the mission of the missile and, particularly, involve thermal cycling (-54 to 65°C is typical for air-launched missiles) which induces stress/strain in the propellant, especially when it is bonded to a rigid structure having a lower thermal expansion coefficient. This is why the first step of the preliminary design analysis is a rough structural analysis, particularly for case-bonded grains.

The second most important mechanical load for the grain is a consequence of the pressure rise at firing. In a case-bonded grain an intense stress field will develop in the propellant due to the case deformation (the propellant is incompressible). In a free-standing grain, if the pressure can equilibrate properly between the port and the interstice between the motor and the grain, the level of stresses will be limited. In any case these stresses will be maximum at ignition when the volumetric loading coefficient is maximum. Also they will be generally maximum at the lowest operating temperature because of the modulus of the propellant even if the

End burning grains. The end-burning configuration is not well adapted to case-bonded architecture because of structural integrity if the propellant is directly bonded to the case. However, it is possible to design such case-bonded grains using stress-relieving grain support and retention systems. These allow thermal shrinkage due to propellant cooling after curing and to thermal cycles and enable pressure to equilibrate like in a free-standing grain configuration during firing.

### . Free-standing grains

Because they are not bonded to the case wall, except sometimes locally, for instance, at the forward end, they allow configurations which cannot be obtained with case-bonded grains (for instance, internal-external burning tube).

Cylindrical configurations. Star, wagon wheel, and tube configurations similar to those already described may be found for free-standing grains. Grain ends are generally simpler: they may be plane.

Configurations with evolving port cross section (trumpet). This configuration was developed to reach high-volume loading fractions for free-standing grains: the cross section of the central port is circular in the forward section and becomes progressively star shaped in the aft section of the grain (Fig. 10).

End-burning grains. The orientation of burning is totally in the longitudinal direction. This configuration has some advantages: gas generation rate is almost constant, volumetric loading fraction is high, and grain manufacture is easy. Side and forward end faces are inhibited. Burning times are long and thrust levels are low or moderate. Thermal insulations and inhibitory play an important role to protect the chamber walls from the long exposure to hot gas and to restrict the combustion to the desired area, respectively. They also generate a high level of pyrolysis gaseous products during firing which must be taken into account in the

phase of the flight of some missiles.

Other type of geometries can be found in some cases: rod and shell, cruciform, wrapped spirals, etc.

# . General Principles for Selection of Grain Configuration

For selection of grain configuration, the main factors which are taken into account are 1) volume available for the propellant grain; 2) grain length-to-diameter ratio (I/d); 3) grain diameter-to-web thickness ratio (d/e); 4) thrust vs time curve, which gives a good idea of what should be the burning area vs web burned curve

# Composite Modified Cast Double-Base Propellants

Composite modified cast double-base (CMDB) are derived from CDB propellants by addition of RDX, HMX and, possibly, nitroglycerine in the casting powder.

# 4. Elastomeric Modified Cast Double-Base Propellants (EMCDB)

Elastomeric modified cast double-base (EMCDB) propellants are produced by the same process as CDB and CMDB with the same type of basic formulations. A hydroxyl prepolymer (polyester, polycaprolactone) and an isocyanate crosslinking agent are introduced in the liquid casting solvent.

These four types of propellant families may be filled with energetic solids (RDX, HMX) but are nowadays generally used without any solid mineral oxidizers or fuels as fillers. In this way their formulation, based on C, H, O, N only, gives them "minimum smoke," sometimes called smokeless, characteristics since there are very few condensed species in the nozzle exhausts for the most generally encountered atmospheric conditions.

# Composite Propellants

These are based on a nonenergetic polymeric binder (generally polybutadiene or polyurethane) and on ammonium perchlorate (AP). They may or may not contain

aluminum powder as a fuel. They are obtained by mixing the solids and the liquid ingredients of the binder, casting, and curing. The propellants without aluminum are reduced-smoke propellants, i.e., there is only secondary smoke formation, by condensation of water with hydrochloric gas resulting from the combustion of AP, in certain conditions of ambient atmosphere temperature and humidity.

## High-Energy Propellants

These are based on a binder highly plasticized by a liquid nitric ester or a mixture of nitric esters, on a nitramine (RDX or HMX). They also may contain anmonium perchlorate and aluminum. These are called cross-linked double base (XLDB) even if there is very little or no nitrocellulose in the binder and a very high level of energetic solids in the formulation which give them a physical behavior of the same type as composite propellants. Their production process is roughly the same as composite propellants. According to the precise formulation they can fall in the minimum-smoke (binder + nitramine), reduced-smoke (binder + AP) or smoky (binder + AP + HMX + Al) category.

Besides the main ingredients, propellants may contain several other ingredients, generally at low contents, used as stabilizers, afterburning suppressants, combustion instabilities suppressants, and burning rate modifiers. One of the important tasks of propellant designers is to find a practical way (fillers particle size, burning rate modifier, etc.) to control the burning rate and pressure exponent, which are key factors in designing solid propellant grains.

## Mechanical Properties

EDB, CDB, and CMDB propellants are generally stiff with high modulus and low-elongation capability at low temperatures so their use is more or less limited to free-standing grains. EMCDB, composite, and high-energy propellants exhibit a viscoelastic behavior. They are very well suited for case-bonded grain applications because of their mechanical behavior: low modulus and high-elongation capability in a wide temperature range.

# F. Energetic Characteristics

The standard delivered specific impulses and densities of these families are summarized in Fig. 11.

Because sometimes different standard motors and conditions of operations are used to measure the standard delivered specific impulse it may be useful to give the precise values in the conditions of Fig. 11 for a few typical propellants: for a composite propellant (HTPB binder, 68% AP, 20% Al), 245 s; for a reduced-snoke propellant (HTPB binder, 85% AP), 236 s; and for a high-energy propellant (high-energy binder, 10% AP, 46% HMX, 19% Al), 254 s.

# . Burning Rate Characteristics

Limitations of propellant burning rates available for a specific project is one of the most frustrating difficulties for the designer who would prefer the use of the greatest possible range of burning rates.

Current practice is to determine them over specified operational temperature ranges utilizing standard or ballistic evaluation motors which are designed to provide neutral pressure traces with sharp tailoffs in order to obtain combustion characteristics with good precision. More economically efficient methods are

![](_page_167_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 11 Standard specific impulse and density of the main propellant families

being developed to obtain the rate of combustion law over a whole range of pressure by using devices with variable K. The regression rate of the propellant can even be obtained directly by ultrasonic measurements of the thickness of unburned propellant during firing.

The basic combustion characteristics and mechanisms for these propellants have been described by Kuo and Summerfield. The more practical aspects will be described now for the three most used families: EDB, CDB, and composite propellants. As previously stated, it is customary to use an empirical expression of the  $r = ap_n^p$  type for the linear burning rate.

Some propellants show regions where the pressure exponent is markedly reduced (plateau effect) or even negative (mesa effect). These very desirable effects, since they reduce the temperature coefficient sensitivity of the motor (at constant K), are typical of double-base propellants using adequate (lead and copper salts, generally) ballistic modifiers.

The burning rate of composite AP propellants is controlled by the AP particle size and the level of AP in the formulation, fine particle size leading to higher burning rates (and also to higher pressure exponents). Solid (copper chromite,

iron oxide) or liquid (ferrocenic derivatives) additives are commonly used in order to get high rates of combustion. Good pressure exponents (0.5 or lower) can generally be obtained for pressures lower than 20 MPa. It may become a difficult problem for higher pressures. The effect of aluminum when used is generally second order.

With the help of the plateau effect double-base propellants can show very low-temperature coefficients. This is generally more difficult to obtain with the most energetic formulations. Values for composite propellants are generally of the order of  $0.2 \cdot 10^{-2a} \text{C}^{-1}$ .

Figure 12 shows the burning rate range, at a given pressure of 7 MPa or at the middle of the plateau for double-base propellants, for the most commonly used propellants. When seeking the highest burning rates, the answer lies with composite propellants for the usual burning times for missile propulsion stages (several seconds to several tens of seconds). However, for very short burning times, on the order of a few tens of milliseconds, which are often used for light antitank missiles where a low signature is generally required, EDB propellants (solventless) are a prime solution because of their physical properties lending themselves particularly well to designs with thin webs (fraction of a millimeter).

For very long burning times, solutions can be found in every family, although they are always accompanied by a significant decrease in the energy characteristics.

![](_page_167_Figure_14.jpeg)

Fig. 12 Standard specific impulse and rate of combustion of the main propellant families.

"Average" (6–16 mm/s at 7 MPa) burning rates can be obtained with every type of propellant. It is useful to know, however, that EDB propellants offer greater burning rates than the CDBs for an equal level of energy.

This is a characteristic inherent in the product. In applications where a large range of operating temperatures is required, the lower temperature coefficient of the homogenous propellants may compensate for low specific impulse per unit volume. With an identical burning rate at 20°C, the propellant with the highest temperature coefficient results in a higher maximum working pressure of the grain at high temperatures, necessitating a thicker structure for the motor, resulting in a weight increase. Similarly, the decreased flow rate at low temperatures can have negative effects because of the resultant reduction in thrust.

Ballistic evaluation motor burning rates must frequently be scaled up by a few percents to correlate with full-scale motor rates. Also, some effects related to the casting process of the grain can sligtly modify the burning rate characteristics of the propellant. All these factors have to be taken into account for a good motor operation prediction. Their influence is generally estimated with the help of data gathered through past experience or specifically designed research programs.

The possible error on the real burning rate of a new propellant grain design is probably the most important factor influencing the precision of the thrust or pressure curve prediction of a new motor. Because past experience is important, the relevant data is often considered proprietary by the organizations involved in the design.

# H. Hazard and Vulnerability Characteristics

Another important feature that must be considered is the hazard characteristics of the propellant. From a legal point of view the most important is the hazard classification which regulates transport, storage, and use of explosive materials and systems incorporating them. According to the U.N. recommendation they are classified in the divisions of Table 4.

The most widely used test to classify propellants is the card-gap test, which measures the ability of a propellant to detonate when initiated by a shock wave. It consists of an explosive donor, an attenuating material, and the acceptor energetic material (propellant). The attenuator, generally made of PMMA cards, is adjusted in thickness to change the shock level to the acceptor confined in a steel sleeve. A mild steel witness plate is used to determine whether or not a detonation occurred. In the U.S. an energetic material is given a 1.1 classification if the acceptor detonates when the gap is 70 cards or greater at the Naval Ordnance Laboratory (NOL) card-gap test. According to this test, composite propellants would be classified as 1.3, and high-energy propellants as 1.1; EDB, CDB, CMDB, and EMCDB are classified as 1.3 for 1.1 depending on the precise formulation (nitroglycerine and nitramine levels). Caution must be exercised when comparisons are made between different countries because different limits between 1.3 and 1.1 may be considered by local authorities even if the same, or nearly the same, test is used for classification.

Insensitive munitions requirements may have also to be considered even if part of the solutions are on the architecture and general design side. If slow cookoff is considered, composite AP propellants will often not pass the test, but they will easily pass the sympathetic detonation test when this will be much more difficult for EDB, CDB, CMDB, and EMCB.

# Production and Cost Characteristics

EDB grains obtained by extrusion are clearly limited in their mass and size. These limitations are mainly due to the size and the performance of the presses that can be used. Diameters are generally limited to 250 mm in the western world. Similarly, only cylindrical grains simple to manufacture and, therefore, inexpensive can be produced.

The EDB process, on the other hand, lends itself particularly well to industrial production as well as to high-production rates. It is, therefore, particularly suited for the production of small ammunitions. The homogeneity of this product and its high degree of rigidity, allowing the production of very thin webs as well as the possibility of machining within very precise limits (a few hundredths of a millimeter), makes it very useful when seeking high-precision impulses. In addition, the combination of rigidity and very thin webs is attractive for grains combining short combustion times with high accelerations, useful for light antitank missiles.

2) When elementary propellant or propellant to liner bonding samples are subjected to aging, they must be as representative as possible of the propellant that will be industrially manufactured and cast into motors.

3) Not only nondestructive and firing tests must be performed on the aged motors but also detailed and analytic evaluations. It is these evaluations alone that provide the capability of creating models of the evolution of the margin of safety throughout the service life, as various properties of the propellant and bonds change.

Experience has shown that the evolution of the ballistic properties of the propellant through aging is generally not very significant except in cases where migration of mobile species (liquid plasticizers or burning rate modifiers) may occur. Most of the failures observed in current practice are related to debonding of the propellant or oxidative aging if proper care is not taken.

# IV. Ballistic Analysis and Grain Design

The grain design will use the propellants and grains characteristics previously described to elaborate the best possible solution to the requirements through a ballistic analysis based on the equations presented in Scc. II.E.

# Requirements on Solid Propellant Grains

The general requirements just described must be transformed into requirements for the propellant grain in order to conduct its design.

# 1. Requirements Related to Functional Specifications

The main internal ballistics requirements are generally of the following types: 1) average, minimum, and maximum values of chamber pressure; 2) thrust, total impulse, and burning times specified within the full operating temperature range; and 3) envelopes of thrust time or mass flow rate vs time curves.

Other requirements are necessary to the designer in order to define a satisfactory propellant grain; maximum weight of propellant grain, maximum axial and transverse acceleration undergone by the propellant grain during operation of the rocket motor, rocket spin rate (for instance for unguided rockets), and dispersions on pressure, thrust, total impulse, burning time have to be specified. Depending on the corresponding requirements, manufacturing process and control operations may be stongly affected and thus the cost of the grain as well. Plume characteristics (emission and transmission in the visible, infrared, electromagnetic wavelength range) are also requirements necessary to define a satisfactory grain.

# 2. Requirements Related to Operational Specifications

Depending on environmental conditions, definition of the propellant grain may be significantly affected. Such conditions must, therefore, be well defined in order to be correctly taken into account during the grain structural design phase.

Long term storage. Desired shelf life, related temperature cycles, and storage conditions must be defined. Particular conditions (e.g., relative humidity, atmosphere in the motor) which could directly affect propellant behavior must be specified.

Thermal environmental conditions. The nature and number of thermal cycles undergone by missiles (for instance, during operational flights for airborne missiles) must be defined. Generally, they are the limiting factors for structural grain design because very low temperatures may be encountered.

Acceleration, handling, and transportation. Acceleration before and during rocket motor operation, i.e., longitudinal acceleration undergone by the rocket motor, must be specified, as well as radial acceleration due to rocket spin which can influence the burning rate of the propellant. Additionally, handling and transportation, i.e., dynamic loads such as shocks and vibrations encountered during

handling (drops) and transportation, must also be specified. Reliability. A level of reliability is more and more commonly required. It is essential to define in which conditions it has to be satisfied and what part of the quantitative reliability objective is allocated to the propellant.

Maintenance. The content and the planning of missile surveillance, inspection, and maintenance must be defined, in so far as they may have an effect on the

rocket motor environmental conditions.

Safety and vulnerability. These requirements may induce an a priori selection of a type of propellant (e.g., a nondetonable propellant or a propellant having a large critical detonation diameter) or, during engineering development, the performance

## Interface Specifications

of safety and vulnerability tests.

Case geometry and properties. A blueprint of the case, or at least, its geometry (length, diameter, configuration of head and aft ends) is mandatory in order to perform grain preliminary design analysis. Physical and mechanical characteristics of the case have a direct effect on structural and ballistic design: type of case, thermal expansion, hoops and longitudinal strains as function of internal pressure, maximum allowable peak pressure (depending on ultimate elastic elongation of case wall), maximum temperature allowable at case wall at the end of motor firing etc.

Thermal insulations. The nature and geometry of thermal insulations must be determined in order to settle the grain definition, either from a ballistic point of view or from a structural point of view. Compatibility between the propellant and the insulation materials must be checked.

Nozzle. The characteristics of the nozzle dictate the practical ballistic performance of a rocket motor. The following characteristics are of particular interest to the grain designer: number and orientation of the nozzles (the angle between nozzle center line and rocket motor center line must be known), degree of nozzle submergence, erosion of the nozzle (diameter evolution) vs operation time at throat and exit planes, angle of the exit cone (or a dimensioned sketch, in the case of a contoured nozzle), failure pressure of the frangible closure disk (it allows the definition of the ignition system and the control of pressurization at ignition), and dimensions of the blast pipe (between chamber and nozzle), when existing; it affects rocket motor efficiency.

Ignition system. The conditions of propellant grain ignition depend on its configuration (location, volume, design) including pressure at the end of ignition and pressurization rate (which affects structural integrity during firing).

Minimum and maximum values of delivered pressure and pressurization rate must be accurately known because they are important factors governing grain structural integrity. An envelope of ignition pressure vs time is of interest for this task.

the initial burning area and of every section of the propellant grain. The computing These various codes require the description (expressed in plane coordinates) of

![](_page_173_Picture_19.jpeg)

![](_page_173_Picture_20.jpeg)

Fig. 14 Two-dimensional geometry evolutions.

time can be adjusted according to the level of accuracy desired. Because of the rapidity at which the computations can be done, a visual display of the computed burning areas is possible. As a rule, the level of accuracy is excellent. In a more complex case where the local burning rate of the propellant is not assumed to be independent of the curvilinear abscissa, the evolution of the burning area as a function of time can be computed with the help of a specially designed computerized numerical model.

Three-dimensional geometry computer codes. These codes (Fig. 15) allow the calculation of burning area of complex configurations, for example, finocyl grains having one or several slots. A first method, limited to the assumption of a constant burning rate throughout the whole grain, uses for each computation step the principle of generation of a surface at a constant distance from the initial surface. This software requires the generation of an initial volumetric grid with a density suitable for the level of accuracy required in the highly three-dimensional zones. A grid generation can only be performed by an expert, and the analysis of the evolution of the grain burning area, in spite of the existence of grid generation preprocessed data, represents a significant amount of work.

Another method, which allows the burning rate to be a variable function of time and space, uses automatic grid generation and management of the burning area evolution. The assessment of the perpendicular for each triangle of the grid is done using a numerical model using hyperbolic nonlinear differential equations.

### Burning Rates

Propellant burning rates are sensitive to several factors.

Pressure. In the pressure range in which rocket motors operate, the power law is generally used. It is also possible to directly use plots of actually measured burning rates vs pressure. The lower the pressure exponent, the more stable the rocket motor internal ballistics.

Temperature. Environmental and use conditions of rocket motors may correspond to a wide temperature range. It is, therefore, necessary to know burning rate sensitivity to initial propellant temperature.

Acceleration. Propellant burning rate is sensitive to acceleration directed normal and into the surface, but it is taken into account only when it is more than 10 g.

Manufacture process. The "hump" effect" is the result of a change in burning rate as a function of web burned (chhancement of burning rate in radially burning grains in the zone between the central port and the motor case walls). It is related to the manufacturing process. Empirical correlations, drawn from experience, are generally applied to take into account this phenomenon in ballistic design. This effect and other causes of ballistic variations will be detailed later.

Internal flow. In the motor the flow of combustion products interact with the propellant combustion phenomena and may locally change the burning rate law which is no longer the one expected. Because of the significant effect of this phenomenon, it is discussed in more detail in the following section.

# Pressure and Thrust Laws

Burning rate laws, evolution of burning surface vs web burned, and basic internal ballistics equations discussed earlier provide pressure vs time and thrust vs time evolutions. In the simple case where internal flow does not significantly

![](_page_174_Picture_16.jpeg)

interact with burning rate, Eqs. (1) and (2) combined with  $\dot{r} = de/dt$ , lead to a differential equation that is numerically solved and that provides web vs time e(t), burning area vs time A(t), pressure vs time p(t), and thrust vs time F(t).

### Effect of Internal Flow

It is often assumed that the flow velocity in the central port exit plane is low enough so that it can be neglected in internal ballistics analysis. It is then assumed that flow is accelerated only in the convergence zone of the nozzle so that it reaches sonic velocity at the nozzle throat. In fact, this assumption is not satisfactory because flow calculations demonstrate that velocities of the order of 100–150 m/s are observed in port exit planes after complete ignition and pressurization. Depending on grain configuration and on propellant properties, two types of phenomenon may be generated: a pressure drop between forward and aft-end of the central port and a local increase of propellant burning rate due to erosive burning.

When performing a ballistic design analysis, one has to quickly assess the magnitude of the phenomena connected with internal flow. Table 7 summarizes the knowledge empirically acquired in this field as the result of numerous solid propellant grain design analyses. This table involves two important factors  $K_p$  and J:

$$J = K_p/K$$
  $K_p = A/A_c$   $K = A_p/A_c$
