where  $A_c$  is the area of a given cross section of the central port, A the propellant burning area upstream of the cross section,  $A_p$  the propellant grain burning area, and  $A_t$  the nozzle throat area.  $K_p$  is directly related to the mass flow rate per unit area G.

$$\rho_p \dot{r} K_p = \rho_p \dot{r} A / A_c = G$$

J gives an idea of the maximum gas velocity (as a rough approximation J=M, the Mach number in the aft end port section) and of the difficulties of the internal ballistic design.

High values, even J=1 (nozzleless motors) can be used if the geometry of the grain is adapted to high-pressure drops along this axis, highly erosive burning and a critical ignition phase.

Pressure drop is related to a decrease of pressure from grain head end to grain aft end. It induces an increase of head-end pressure at the first phase of motor firing and, therefore, the maximum pressure generally increases. Pressure drops are generally due to energy losses inside the flow and to phenomena occurring at the interface of flow and propellant surface or to sharp changes of port section or of flow direction, and to side injections from burning propellant walls.

One of the critical steps in rocket motor operation, therefore, occurs just after ignition when port sections (through which combustion gas must flow) are minimum. Average pressure drops values encountered are of the order of 0.1 MPa between head and aft end. In some cases, for special configurations, pressure drops of more than 1 MPa have been observed.

A gaseous flow is fully characterized by the knowledge of local velocities, pressures, and temperatures. Computer codes have been developed in order to know such characteristics. They are used to describe unsteady phases during pressure rise at ignition and to know steady flow just after ignition, in the whole cavity and in the nozzle convergence section.

To use these codes, one has to generate a grid of the combustion chamber. Ordern symmetry (when existing) is taken into account so as to reduce the analysis to a sector of  $\epsilon \pi / n$  (n = symmetry number). Results may be presented either as gas velocity or pressure contour line in various cavity sections (Fig. 16) or as curves representing for instance gas velocity as a function of radial distance from the central axis in the central port cross section.

### . Erosive Burning

Enhancement of propellant burning rate due to tangential gas flow (compared to propellant burning rate without tangential flow) is known as erosive burning. It occurs when the propellant burning surface is subjected to a high-velocity combustion gas flow parallel to it. The value of  $K_{\rho}$  of the chosen design gives

![](_page_175_Picture_21.jpeg)

Fig. 16 Gas velocity field, Mach number representation

a rough idea of the presence of erosive burning. The phenomenon is due to an increase of heat transfer from the flame zone to the propellant surface. There are numerous physical models to explain and to quantify this phenomenon.

One-dimensional models are generally well suited to compute internal ballistic of large l/d tactical motors with smooth changes in cross section along the axis. The accuracy of such models (within 5% on peak pressure at ignition) allows the design of grains with high J factors and high level of erosive burning in the aft-end region.

Predictive and accurate erosive burning models are available today for composite propellants, but a lack still remains for nitramine based (CMDB, XLDB) propellants which seem to be experimentally far less sensitive (Fig. 17). On the other hand, higher pressure exponents which may be observed with this family of propellants impose on the designers a higher accuracy in pressure drop predictions.

Two-dimensional models need more sophisticated flow solvers which cannot yet take into account erosive burning in complex geometries. Fortunately, axisymetrical configurations, with high-internal-flow velocities, are not frequently met or can often be treated with one-dimensional computer codes.

In the case of three-dimensional geometries, the results of flow calculations can only be analyzed to point at zones with erosive burning occurence risk but still fail in taking into account coupling between flow and combustion. Designers, in that case, must evaluate mass flow rate increase and generally take a higher safety margin for predicted ignition pressure.

As an example, a simple computer code, based on a one-dimensional flow model, allows the calculation head-end and aft-end pressure evolutions in a grain experiencing erosive burning. The erosive burning law selected for the model is

$$\dot{r}_{i} = \dot{r}_{0}[1 + (G - G_{0})]$$

where  $r_e$  is the burning rate with erosive burning,  $r_0$  the burning rate without erosive burning, G the mass flow rate per unit area in the given port cross section and  $G_0$  is the mass flow rate per unit area threshold (beyond which erosive burning occurs).

This law may be experimentally established for a precise case or may be the result of modeling work for some well-known propellants like AP composites.

This type of code is used systematically in preliminary ballistic design analysis. It implies that grain geometry be described by the cross section contour perimeter evolution along the grain axis. Erosive burning is calculated in several cross sections of the central port according to local flow characteristics (static pressure p and local mass flow rate G) and to the preceeding erosive burning law. The ignition phase is simulated as an unsteady phenomenon; time steps range from 1 to 5 ms. A complete motor firing may be simulated, using a steady-state model and time steps generally ranging from 0.05 to 0.1 s (Fig. 18). A more comprehensive investigation of erosive burning in propellant grains, though keeping a one-dimensional geometry assumption, may be performed with more sophisticated codes. 12

Erosive burning is quite often a problem in tactical motors because of high length-to-diameter ratios and high-volumetric loadings. Predictive models are becoming more and more reliable, but some surprises can still happen at the first firing of a new design. On the other hand, more and more designs incorporate voluntarily erosive burning. Since, quite generally, high thrust is required at the very beginning of the firing, erosive burning can supply the needed supplement of thrust (and pressure) that the burning surface evolution does not offer alone.

# 10. Motor Ballistic Characteristic Predictions, Ballistic Anomalies

A systematic analysis of burn time variations in solid rocket motors has been recently presented. <sup>13</sup> Propellant weight, propellant density, and characteristic velocity variations have a small effect (0.1–0.2%) on the burn time variations. Throat area variations have a slightly more pronounced effect, especially for small motors with high throat erosion. The main influence factors are the burn surface area variations (due to variations in the geometry of the propellant grain with production, for instance, port ovality or port misalignment) and the burning rate variations.

The pressure inside the combustion chamber cavity is assumed to be

$$\frac{p'}{p_0} = \sum_{i=1}^n e^{\alpha_i t} e^{j\omega_i t} \psi_i(M) \qquad (j^2 = -1)$$

where  $p_0$  is the average chamber pressure, p' the instantaneous pressure at point M,  $\omega_i$  the pulsation of mode of rank i and of frequency  $f_i$ ,  $\psi_i$  the spatial form of mode of rank i, M the point in grain cavity, and  $\alpha_i$  the damping coefficient (when  $\alpha_i < 0$ ), or gain factor (when  $\alpha_i > 0$ ) of the mode of rank i.

The first step of the analysis consists of calculating the various acoustical modes which are specific to the grain cavity. Finite element methods are generally used. Examples of grids and corresponding results are presented in Figs. 19 and 20. The second step of the analysis consists of calculating the value of  $\alpha$  (equal to the algebric sum of the various gain and damping factors). These calculations need

## ۲. General Procedure for a Propellant Grain-Design Optimization and Computer-Aided Preliminary Design

### P. General Description

as early as the preliminary design phase, quick and rather accurate results defining To optimize the motor and missile design, there is an increasing pressure to have,

![](_page_180_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 21 General structure for the first two modes

the propellant grain. As an example, a computer code allowing satisfaction of these needs will be presented. Figure 21 presents the general architecture of the code. Each type of grain configuration (star-shaped, slotted tube, axisymmetric, finocyl, etc.) is individually treated inside the code. The procedure for any of these configurations is the one which is generally followed to perform propellant grain preliminary design analysis. The architecture of the code is modular so that any addition of new modules or any improvement of an existing module may be very simply worked out.

Runs are controlled by the user from the graphic terminal. CPU time is negligible

Runs are controlled by the user from the graphic terminal. CPU time is negligible as compared to time spent by the user performing the design analysis.

The process is iterative and, besides the input of technical specifications, the user has only to answer yes or no to the option which is proposed on the screen. Results are presented either as tables or as curves. The diagram presented in Fig. 21 emphasizes the role of propellant/configuration selection, which allows one to provide several possibilities, ranked according to a given set of criteria. The selection of propellant/configuration depends on the one hand, on technical requirements (total impulse, burning time, etc.), or on the other hand, on semiquantitative requirements related, for instance, to manufacturing process praticality, industrial and economical aspects, etc.

Because of the dual nature of the criteria, Expert Systems has been selected and implemented for this critical stage of preliminary design analysis.

# B. Description of the Code

All of the branches of the code have the same basic structure. The slotted tube branch is detailed hereafter. On the flow chart of Fig. 22, the main stages of the analysis appear. Two possibilities are provided to the user: design of a case-bonded (or free-standing) grain meeting technical requirements or for a given rocket motor, calculation of motor operation (pressure vs time, thrust vs time, etc.).

![](_page_180_Figure_12.jpeg)

Fig. 22 Detailed flow chart of a slotted tube propellant grain design computation

In the case of slotted tube configurations, geometrical characteristics which are taken into account for design analysis are the cylindrical motor case, presenting possibly thermal insulation over-thickness in the aft-end zone (slotted zone); the plane grain aft-end zone (slotted zone); the cylindrical central port; the slot walls, which may be parallel or not (star shaped); and the possibility of a tapered port in grain aft zone (to limit erosive burning effects).

The successive steps of the analysis are as follows.

The first step is definition of the grain and thermal structural analysis. This definition meets various requirements, the priorities of which are ranked as 1) structural integrity (for thermally induced strains) corresponding to a required minimum safety factor, 2) maximum operating pressure, 3) total impulse, 4) evolution of pressure vs time, and 5) burning time. The structural analysis is based on data obtained from regression analysis of stress/strain field determination results, performed with the aid of three-dimensional computer codes on various selected slotted tube geometries.

Burning area evolution calculation is the second step, followed by determination of nozzle throat initial diameter. It is the minimum throat diameter value consistent with the specification on maximum acceptable pressure.

The fourth step is determination of pressure, mass flow rate, and thrust evolutions vs time. In a first approach, erosive burning is not taken into account. The calculations provide expansion ratio and, thus, nozzle optimum expansion ratio and exit diameter. Nozzle exit diameter is then compared to corresponding requirement. Afterwards, ratios K and J are calculated. If needed, 1 tapered zone is designed in the grain slots region so as to meet a criterion on J (maximum permitted value). Burning area vs time is then calculated again.

The next step is the definition of equivalent axisymmetric longitudinal port contour. It is based on equal flow rates in any port cross section for actual (three dimensional) and equivalent (two-dimensional axisymmetric) contours. It allows a simplified analysis of erosive burning which is taken into account at the following stage.

The sixth step is calculation of head-end and aft-end pressure evolution. At this stage erosive burning is taken into account. The module provides pressure evolution inside the grain central port, as well as peak pressure at ignition and pressure rise time.

The last step is prediction of the safety factor related to ignition pressurization.

## VI. Some Specific Cases

Probably 90% of the cases practically encountered are included in the preceding developments. There are, however, some special applications that require special configurations or designs, such as, nozzleless motors, end burners, and pulse motors.

## A. Nozzleless Motors

The nozzleless motor is an early design that resulted from an analysis of the conventional rocket motor, demonstrating that the nozzle accounted for a significant portion of the cost, the weight, and the size of a rocket motor.

The propellant grain of a nozzleless motor is usually case bonded with a generally cylindrical central port, connected at the aft end to an exit cone molded in the propellant (Fig. 23). The combustion pressure will decrease with time because of

![](_page_181_Picture_17.jpeg)

Fig. 23 Nozzleless configuration

the absence of a throat. In the absence of nozzle throat, the operating conditions of the nozzleless rocket motor are governed by an aerodynamic constriction of the gas flow: The exit cone section at the aft end of the grain is designed to allow gas expansion, thereby improving performance.

The development of nozzleless rocket motors is linked to composite propellants with high-burning rates, because this type of grain requires a minimum flow rate to ensure a stable performance, in turn, requiring prohibitive length-to-diameter ratios when other propellant compositions are used. In addition, the propellant pressure exponent should be less than 0.5 in order to obtain designs which are competitive with nozzled motor configurations. Length over diameter ratios over five are generally used.

The main advantages of nozzleless rocket motors are simplicity of the propeliant grain geometry, greater loading ratio than in a conventional rocket motor, weight reduction due to absence of mechanical parts at the aft end and absence of nozzle, significant decrease of the cost of the rocket motor, and elimination of a nozzle which otherwise would have to be ejected at the end of the acceleration phase, making this concept very interesting for use as an integral booster for rocket-ramjet or ducted-rocket systems. In fact the first real applications (advanced development programs) until today were conducted on ducted rockets.

There are some drawbacks as the price to pay for these advantages. There is a loss of approximately 15–20% of specific impulse in comparison to a conventional system depending on the geometry and optimization of the grain. There is a danger of combustion instabilities occuring at low pressure. There is a need to have a thorough knowledge of the normal burning rate and erosive combustion for a very large range of pressures, to enable performance predictions. Furthermore, the propellant grain mechanical deformation is directly reflected in the ballistics of the motor.

Although nozzleless motors have much lower specific impulse values than conventional motors using the same propellant, this loss can be largely compen-

### Design Problems

Considering all of the potential advantages of the nozzleless motor one may wonder why there have not been more applications today. There is, of course,

of this design that may explain the fact that it has been used today only for integral boosters for ducted rockets or ramjets where they have a clearly defined advantage are the necessity of high length to diameter ratios; the impossibility, of course, to use blast tubes and the consequence on the missile architecture; the possible variations of the thrust vector angle depending of the variations of the geometry of the propellant (bore and divergent part) during production; the impossibility to realize two level thrust regimes typical of tactical systems; the thrust levels that are minimum at the beginning of firing, and the difficult accurate performance predictions. If the total impulse can be predicted quite accurately, the predicted initial values for thrust and pressure can be wrong by 10%. Part of the problem is due to the coupling between ballistics and mechanical deformation of the propellant already mentioned. After some firings of a given design and adaptation of parameters of the model, computations can be much more precise for propellant grains of the same family.

### End Burners

An end-burning grain configuration is not well suited for case bonding, and end burners were first developed as free-standing, completely inhibited (except the aft face) grains, bonded or attached to the case at the forward end. A careful structural analysis of this area must be done. It is also generally necessary to have some devices to support the grain at the aft end to allow for thermal dilatations. Care must be taken with the aerodynamic alimentation of the interstice between the inhibitor and the case. If the pressure rise was not fast enough, there would be, especially with low modulus propellant (composites), compression of the grain in the forward end of the case due to the pressure on the aft face and possible ruptures of the inhibitor or the propellant in that zone.

These types of problems can be solved by using thermal stress releasing thermal insulation boots made of longitudinal alveolus able to open under thermal cycles and bonded to the propellant on one face and to the case on the other face. The same concept can be used more simply, but with may be less reliability, by bonding the grain inhibitor to the case on a few generatrices.

# Burning Rate Enhancement Along the Inhibitor

Most of the problems encountered with the design of end-burning grains deal with the observation of progressive pressure and thrust curves observed at firing when a neutral curve was expected, due to numerous burning rate enhancement phenomena possible at the propellant-inhibitor interface. The consequence is a burning surface area increase or "coning" phenomenon.

Six or seven différent mechanisms have been described. Some of them are general and can be observed with any type of propellant and inhibitors. Some of them are specific of composites, others of nitrate-esters plasticized propellants. The burning rate increase along the inhibitor may be the result of heating of the propellant under the inhibitor due to the circulation of hot gases in the interstice during a long burning time. It may result from segregation of the fine oxidizer particles in the vicinity of the inhibitor. This possibility exists only in the case of composite propellants cast in their inhibitors. It may result from the existence in the inhibitor of solids that are burning rate accelerators (fron oxide for instance) or

the existence of porosities or microscopic cracks, increasing the burning rate (for instance, if the stress relief boot is not carefully designed and the local strain of the propellant is very high). It may result from alteration of the local composition of the propellant through the migration of mobile molecules toward the inhibitor, and its reciprocal (active or inactive plasticizers, cross linking agents, liquid combustion catalysts, and others) or from localized variations in the radiation density of the flame in the vicinity of the inhibitor.

For instance, if the plasticizer of a propellant can migrate to the inhibitor, the propellant close to the inhibitor appears, locally, richer in oxidizing species. This results in an increase in the burning rate as well as in the pressure exponent, so that the burning rate enhancement grows with the temperature and operating pressure of the motor.

To minimize this phenomenon, the grain designer selects materials that are impermeable to the mobile components of the propellants, and balances, a priori, the chemical potentials on each side of the bonding plane. When equilibrium cannot be obtained, he may, on the other hand, introduce into the inhibitor a plasticizer moderating the burning rate of the propellant.

### Design Issues

As already seen, most design issues deal with the design of the inhibitor and the interstice between the grain inhibitor and the case (or the thermal insulation of the case).

Aerodynamic problems in the interstice. The first of the two mains problems is related to the alimentation by hot gases that cool down progressively. An example is given at Fig. 25. This temperature elevation can be the cause of burning rate enhancement and the inhibitor thickness must be designed accordingly. The second is related to a gas circulation that can lead to high ablation rates of the inhibitor in the aft part of the grain when the space between case and grain is high, and the grain is supported by its lower generatrix. This can be solved by the longitudinal partial case bonding already described or an increase of the thickness of the inhibitor in the aft part. Since it has been shown that gases circulate in a length approximately

### Nozzle Design

B. H. Prescott\*
Thiokol Corporation and
Michael Macocha<sup>†</sup>
Morton International

### I. Overview

NGST tactical solid rocket motors are cost-performance driven requiring maximum performance from the nozzle and other components within very restrictive geometric envelopes and at the lowest costs possible.

Maximizing nozzle performance requires optimizing motor ballistics (i.e., pressures and burn times), exit cone thrust, and weight to meet mission requirements for the entire missile system. Fulfilling mission requirements may require a simple fixed nozzle configuration or multiple (2–4) highly complex movable nozzles employing noneroding throats and thrust vector control (TVC) concepts. In addition to providing motor ballistic and mission performance capabilities, the nozzle design must also meet environmental and system life requirements with a highly reliable design.

Because other monographs have been written discussing solid rocket motor nozzles in general, this portion of Chapter 6 will address only those design issues unique to tactical nozzles, specifically, basic nozzle function, key design features and operating conditions, nozzle types, design considerations, materials, TVC systems, and design process. Examples of typical production or development systems will be incorporated wherever possible.

## A. Basic Nozzle Function

The basic function of the nozzle is to 1) direct subsonic motor gases, 2) provide a ballistic throat, and 3) provide additional motor thrust. The nozzle throat diameter (i.e., area) inversely affects motor operating pressures as noted by the

# 3. Design Features and Operation Conditions

A nozzle consists of three distinct regions: 1) the inlet or entrance where subtionic motor gases are directed into the throat, 2) the throat where choked flow maintains motor chamber pressure, and 3) the exit cone where supersonic gases are efficiently expanded and controlled to obtain additional thrust. Typical external and submerged nozzle schematics are shown in Fig. 1.

Operating temperatures, pressures, and gas velocities (Mach numbers) vary widely from region to region, as shown in Fig. 2. Pressures equivalent to those n the motor chamber can be as high as 3500 psi in the inlet region decreasing to

# Envelope and Interface Constraints

The nozzle external envelope is determined from the missile's aerodynamic profile constraints, often including a restricted region to package a missile's external aerodynamic fin actuation control system, as noted earlier. To fit within the control system envelope, a long cylindrical blast tube concept is used. The nozzle designer must fit not only the structural components but also internal-insulators inside of the structure to protect it from the high-temperature motor gases as well. The inner diameter of the blast tube insulator must be slightly larger than the throat diameter to prevent nearly sonic flow and subsequent high erosion and performance losses. Blast tubes have been static-tested with Mach numbers as high as 0.8. In addition to these two design challenges, additional structural outer surface temperatures may be restricted to prevent radiant heating of the fin control actuation components. A typical blast tube—exit cone outer diameter temperature requirements profile is shown in Fig. 5.

Occasionally the blast tube—exit cone maximum temperature requirement extends beyond motor burnout as indicated in Fig. 5. This is an additional design challenge since now the nozzle designer must not only provide enough insulation on the inside diameter of the blast tube and exit cone for motor burn conditions, as well as additional insulation to be able to absorb the energy generated in the insulators during motor operation. The postmotor burn requirements are often referred to as heat-soak requirements.

During missile deployed life, access to the fin actuation system requires the nozzle exit cone be removable and replaceable. Such a configuration is shown in Fig. 6. This design illustrates a threaded joint between the blast tube and exit cone structures since this concept requires less radial space than a typical bolted configuration. If the fin actuation system envelope is too restrictive to thread the exit cone onto the aft end of the blast tube, sometimes the threaded interface is moved to the forward end of the blast tube, as illustrated earlier in Fig. 3. This design is not as desirable as the previous concept since it places the threaded joint and o-ring seal in more of a blind area.

### Thrust Vector Control

Incorporation of TVC onto a system involves another set of operational and nonoperational requirements. The TVC system may either employ a fixed nozzle with movable components, such as jet vanes or tabs to deflect the exhaust gases, or a movable flex bearing or trapped ball (TB) nozzle system.

![](_page_187_Picture_12.jpeg)

Fig. 6 Blast tube nozzle with removable-replaceable exit cone.

### Performance

The only nozzle-related performance parameter sometimes specified in systems requirements is a maximum nozzle weight or, more often, natural frequency for movable TVC systems. Otherwise, most other nozzle design requirements are determined from optimizing the overall motor to meet system requirements.

### Reliability Factor

Reliability is usually allocated from overall motor requirements and typically results in a factor like 0.999+ for the nozzle subsystem, either fixed or movable. A reliability factor is attributed to most tactical systems operating in close proximity to personnel or equipment, such as launching from the deck of a ship or from mounts on an aircraft.

## Nonoperating Requirements

Commonly referred to as shipping and handling, these requirements do not typically drive nozzle designs but may require interfacing features to accommodate them. They may include requirements such as electrical grounding, safety factors, temperatures, motor sealings and environmental requirements. Each of these is briefly discussed as follows.

Electrical grounding. Requirements are usually limited to those defined in specification MII.-B-5087, Class S, which require conductive components to be able to bleed off static charge buildup during storage or handling. Most nozzle materials or components that generate or collect static electricity are naturally grounded through physical interfaces such as bolted or threaded joints or physical material interfaces, and so forth. Usually maximum resistance between two components is limited to 1 ohm or less. Typically, nozzles will measure less than 0.5 ohms of resistance between components, excluding some movable nozzle TVC concepts, such as flex bearings or trapped ball designs, which usually are grounded via a motor interface by a grounding strap.

Electrical grounding of nozzle and motor components recently has become a much more serious safety concern to prevent, for example, accidental ignition of the motor's solid propellant during handling. This phenomenon has been referred to as electrostatic discharge or ESD.

|                 |               |               |                |  |

Safety factors. During ground handling by personnel, ultimate structural safety factors can be 2.0 or greater, or 1.5 when in close proximity of aircraft or other critical equipment. These factors apply to nonoperating stresses or loads during handling or shipping caused by high-acceleration loads such as 30 g in an axial direction and/or 60–70 g laterally. These high-acceleration loads result from a motor being dropped or from in-flight conditions on an aircraft.

Temperatures. During shipping, handling, or flight preoperational conditions, nozzle components can be exposed to temperatures from -60°F to as high as +170°F approximately as noted in Table 2

+170°F approximately as noted in Table 2.

Motor sealing. This is usually required for most tactical motors and generally installed somewhere in the nozzle exit cone region. Three types of motor seals are

Illustrated in Fig. 7.

The first example is a prethane foam barrier with a thin aluminum foil moisture barrier on the forward face and an environmental sealant coating on the aft face. This seal is bonded into the forward exit cone region. Valves allowing the motor to breathe can be incorporated if required.

The second example is a simple thin PTFE or polypropylene film barrier trapped between two nozzle components during exit cone installation.

The third example is a more complex structural design consisting of a thin stainless-steel cup with a valve located in the center to allow the motor to breathe under certain delta pressure conditions. This seal is bonded to the aft end of the nozzle exit cone structural shell. All seals must be expelled from the nozzle on ignition without contributing to motor pressure pertubations causing any damage to the nozzle, or leaving any debris in the exit cone creating adverse thrust misalignment.

Environmental. In addition to the temperature extremes discussed earlier, tactical systems are exposed to harsh environments during handling, shipping, and preoperational deployment. Service environments may involve humidity effects from 50 to 100% combined with temperatures of 40–100°F often including salt spray.

# 6. Operating Requirements

During motor operation times, the severe internal pressures, temperatures, and gas species of the propellant combustion process usually overrides any other system induced loads or conditions on the nozzle and its components.

Safety factors. For structural components, safety factor requirements of 1.25–1.50 are typically applied to ultimate conditions, whereas factors of 1.0–1.2 are applied to yield parameters. Occasionally, a nozzle closure or adapter is routinely hydroproofed during manufacturing acceptance. Usually a safety factor of 1.0 is applied to thermal requirements. But, as noted earlier, additional insulator thickness may be required to maintain structural component temperatures below acceptable limits after motor burnout and during subsequent heat soak.

![](_page_188_Picture_15.jpeg)

Fig. 7 Examples of nozzle mounted motor seals.

Thrust misalignment. Thrust misalignment is defined as the maximum allowable offset and angular difference between the motor centerline (defined by centerlines of the motor attach features) and the nozzle centerline (defined as the centerline connecting the center of the throat and the exit cone, exit plane), as shown schematically in Fig. 8. Typically maximum allowable offset requirements are less than 0.020 in. at the throat, whereas maximum allowable angular misalignment values typically range from 1.5 to 3.5 mrad (0.085–0.20 deg). These requirements are usually verified by geometric evaluation of the nozzle and loaded motor design drawings.

If more than two or three interfaces are involved in calculating offset and misalignment, a root-sum-square (RSS) approach may be employed that results in significantly smaller effects on dimensional tolerances.<sup>3</sup> An example of this approach is presented in Fig. 9.

![](_page_189_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 8 Definition of thrust offset and misalignment

### 7. Others

Cost. Costs are often a dominant consideration during nozzle design and/or manufacturing tradeoffs. Tactical motor systems are more often driven to the lowest unit cost, because they usually are required in large quantities.

Environmental. Although not typically specified, nozzle components, materials, or processes involving carcinogenic materials, ozone depleting chemicals (ODCs), or in usage volatile organic compounds (VOCs) have recently become unavailable or are very restricted in usage.

Of prime concern since passage of Public Law 102-484 (June 1993), and President George Bush's mandate February 1992, the solid propulsion industry has been fully aware of the phase-out of certain ODCs and VOCs that were involved in tactical nozzle fabrication. Compounds involved are certain chlorofluorocarbons such as Freon<sup>®</sup>, 1,1,1-trichloroethanes such as methyl chloroform, and trichlorethane (TCA). Carbon tetrachloride also was banned in systems after 1994–1995.

Known carcinogenic compounds used in tactical nozzle materials manufacture such as di-methyl formamide (DMF) will not be allowed on future systems. All asbestos-containing materials have been phased out since the mid-1980s and some slightly radioactive fillers such as Thoria in tungsten alloys will soon be unavailable.\*

Producibility. Producibility involves incorporating concurrent engineering, or total quality management (TQM) practices to involve materials suppliers, manufacturing, quality, and prime contractor personnel early in the design phase to define a producible tactical nozzle. Involving the prime system contractor early ensures that the designer is provided with customer requirements not often spelled out in a systems requirements document.

### III. Materials

Nozzle materials are separated into structural and ablative/insulator components. Current design practices for tactical nozzle structural components include metals, glass, carbon or graphite reinforced composites, or a simple overwrapped ablative insulator with the same or other material at a different ply angle.

Selection of an insulator or ablative material is determined by the gas pressure, temperature, and the presence or lack of reactive gas species, or solid particles in the exhaust gases. An entire volume by itself would be required to cover all nozzle materials, processing, properties, and design techniques so this section will only briefly address those materials as state of the art (SOTA) for tactical nozzles. Tactical nozzles are generally manufactured in high production rates and large lot quantities utilizing low-cost materials and/or processes.

A brief discussion concerning materials tradeoffs and industry standard quality control follows at the end of the materials usage, advantages/disadvantages, and brief manufacturing description.

## A. Structural Materials

Structural components maintain nozzle integrity by supporting the insulators and ablative liners, as well as reacting motor chamber pressure loads and external loads during flight and TVC systems.

Steels are commonly used for structural components because of their relatively low cost, high strength, and modulus. They are used where minimal structural deflections and high load reactions are required, but require corrosion and oxidation protection. High-strength stainless steels, such as 17-4 Ph and 17-7 Ph, are used due to their corrosion resistance, but their costs are higher than carbon steels.

High-strength (75–80 ksi) corrosion resistant aluminum alloys, such as 7075 or 7175, are available that can be used when weight needs to be minimized.

<sup>\*</sup>More details of the aforementioned efforts and updated status can be obtained from the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency's Industrial Toxics Program (33/50 Program).

Additional envelope is required for the slightly thicker aluminum cross sections than steel counterparts for equivalent stiffness or deflections.

High-strength D6AC or 300M steels are often used for nozzle aft closures or structures subjected to the high chamber pressure environment, whereas lower grades of carbon steels can be used for exit cone or attach structures. Military handbook MIL-STD-5 is often used for metals properties and recommended usages. Fracture toughness should be a prime consideration when selecting stainless or carbon steels for nozzle usage. Typical properties for 7075-T6 aluminum, 6AL-4V titanium, A1S1 4130 carbon steel, and a low-strength 200 series stainless steel are summarized on Table 3. Proper protection against stress or galvanic corrosion should also be considered when combining different metals or alloys in structural components.

Titanium alloys are not used very often for tactical nozzles because of cost and availability. Composites are not generally used either because of their higher costs, as well as low interlaminer shear capabilities in high bending or rotational operating modes. Some weight critical designs, however, have used glass epoxy or silica or glass phenolic structures for the exit cones.

Most metallic structures are machined plate, tubing, or bar stock. Forgings or rolled ring forgings offer higher strengths and specifically oriented properties capabilities. Castings are sometimes used on high production rate components where strength and grain orientations are not design drivers. However, castings require more expensive process controls or nondestructive evaluation such as x rays.

### Insulators/Ablatives

Nozzle insulator or ablative materials generally consist of glass, silica, carbon, or graphite fabric reinforced phenolics or fiber reinforced elastomers. Often special erosion-resistant materials such as polycrystalline graphites (bulk graphite), pyrolytic graphite, tungsten (pure or reinforced) or carbon-carbons are used in the throat region.

Carbon and graphite phenolics are made from wood pulp extracts, as shown in Fig. 10. Rayon fibers are woven, carbonized, or graphitized and then impregnated

![](_page_190_Picture_13.jpeg)

Fig. 10 Carbon and graphite cloth phenolic manufacturing.

NOZZLE DESIGN

![](_page_191_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 11 Silica and glass cloth phenolic manufacture.

with a phenolic resin. Sometimes the phenolic resins are filled with carbon black

or other fillers to aid in processing or producing lower composite densities. Glass and silica phenolics are produced from silica sands as shown in Fig. 11. They can also be impregnated with filled or unfilled phenolic resins. Fillers usually consist of silica dioxide powders.

Insulators are often tape wrapped (parallel to or at an angle to the component axis) on metal mandrels and autoclave or hydroclave cured. They can also be molded as flat laminates, "coolie hat" or rosette (also known as involute) layups, or chopped square molding compounds as shown in Fig. 12. Phenolics are typically vacuum bag cured at 325°F for 2 hrs in an autoclave (225 psi) or hydroclave cured (at 1000 psi). Molded parts are generally cured at the same temperature under 1000–1250 psi mold pressures without vacuum requirements.

In all cases the tape, laminates, coolie hat/rosette patterns, or chopped squares are cut from wider broadgoods. Table 4 compares properties of typical fabric reinforced carbon, graphite, silica, and glass phenolic composites.

Carbon or graphite phenolics are usually used for maximum erosion resistance in aluminized propellant environments, whereas silica phenolics perform better as ablatives in nonaluminized environments.

Elastomers are sometimes used in low-velocity regions of the nozzle inlet, such as fixed housing insulators. They are fairly low cost and readily processed by casting directly onto the structure. In a low Mach number region, they offer sufficient insulation and excellent elongation capabilities allowing them to deflect along with the nozzle structure without cracking or becoming unbonded, even at low temperature. Formulations that have been used include nitrile butadiene rubber (NBR), carbon or silica filled EPDM, and silicone rubber.

Other applications for elastomers involve their use as adhesives/sealants. Room temperature vulcanized (RTV) silicone elastomers have been used to bond the phenolic closure insulation components to the shell. RTV's pliability allows large

![](_page_191_Picture_13.jpeg)

Fig. 12 Nozzle ablative/insulator component manufacturing methods.

shell deflections without opening up a gas path or inducing large stresses in the adjoining ablative insulation components.

### C. Throat Materials

Sometimes tactical motors require maximum delivered performance, hence lower eroding (or noneroding) throat materials than silica or carbon phenolics described earlier. These materials are typically polycrystalline or bulk graphites, pyrolytic graphites, three or four directionally reinforced carbon–carbons, or tungsten, as will be described. Table 5 compares properties of typical throat materials and Fig. 13 shows their manufacturing methods.

### Carbon-Carbons

same or higher than bulk graphites and can be made into very stable geometries. Carbon-carbons typically erode slightly higher than graphites and can cost more because of materials and processing costs. Carbon-carbons (C-Cs) are very thermal shock resistant, with densities the

properties at 4000°F.

\*Properties for 90% dense unalloyed pressed and sintered tungsten. aValues shown are room temperature properties except the second value which refers to

![](_page_193_Figure_4.jpeg)

![](_page_193_Figure_5.jpeg)

![](_page_193_Figure_6.jpeg)

Fig. 13 Typical throat materials manufacture.

erosion maintains a more constant motor operating pressure and a higher average nozzle expansion ratio, thereby providing maximum motor performance. A typical tungsten throat may even decrease throat area with burntime, as much as 7% or up to 0.070–0.090 in. on a diameter.

Tungsten is relatively expensive due to fabrication costs and is brittle at temperatures between room temperature and 800°C approximately, and therefore is susceptible to thermal shock cracking. Component strength can be increased by starting with forgings with aligned grain flow for improved strength in the critical directions. Incorporation of an additive, such as thoria, improves thermal shock resistance by lowering the ductile-to-brittle transition temperature and increasing its strain capability at elevated temperatures. Because of its carcinogenic nature, alternatives to thoria, such as carbides, are being evaluated.

Because of tungsten's increased ducility above 1000°F, the insert must be thick enough in cross-section that sufficient material remains supported by other nozzle components to preclude the insert from being extruded out of the nozzle. The larger the cross-section, however, the more susceptible the tungsten is to thermal shock cracking. Recent insert designs have incorporated a series of radial grooves to essentially precrack the insert and reduce the internal axial stresses to allowable levels. Providing support for the hot tungsten insert and sufficient insulation to maintain required nozzle structures temperatures (approximately 200°F) complicates the design with additional costs. Tungsten is seldom justified in any large motor system. However, the loss of performance associated with even small changes to the throat diameter in a tactical sized motor sometimes justifies the added costs.

### Adhesives

Adhesives are used quite often to assemble the various components to themselves and the structural shell. Several families of strength structural adhesives have been utilized in rocket motor nozzles, some of which are epoxies, silicones, polysulfides, and phenolic. The adhesives must have sufficient capability over the full operating temperature range, usually from  $-65^{\circ}$ F to  $+250^{\circ}$ F. Restrictions since the mid-1980s prohibit the use of asbestos, hence the designation NA (nonasbestos) noted on some vendor designations.

Although it is not desirable to rely on adhesives for primary structural loads, they are often used in this capacity in tactical nozzles. High-strength, high-elongation EC-3448 adhesive (from 3M Corp.) and a suitable primer have been used as the primary bond for nozzle exit cones.

Cohesive strength between the adhesive and the substrate is a function of proper surface preparation such as cleaning and wettability of the adhesive. Primers compatible with the adhesive provide surface wetting and can increase bond strengths significantly.

As discussed earlier the nozzle designer must decide on the appropriate materials that will satisfy all design requirements and provide the most efficient and cost-effective solution to the design problem. To compare the relative costs and nozzle cost impacts of different materials is difficult because of changing costs with time. As an example, raw material costs of carbon or graphite phenolics shown in Fig. 14 have increased from the \$50 per pound shown in 1988 to approximately \$75–\$90 per pound in 1994. In addition, the cost is highly dependent on the size, shape, desired strength, consistency, and orientation of the finished component. For example, there are many grades of bulk graphites, with extremely varied costs. These grades not only vary in density but also in material strength, consistency, and overall quality. Likewise, a tape-wrapped carbon phenolic component can cost twice as much as one that is compression molded using chopped squares. Of course, this results in a more

# F. Quality Control Provisions

Nozzle quality is assured by 1) raw material controls, 2) process controls, 3) nondestructive testing, and 4) nondestructive verification components or of completed nozzle assemblies where possible.

Controlling key raw materials properties will ensure that quality components can be manufactured economically with demonstrated processes. Sample testing of components assures that their performance would be in the range of previous demonstrated or qualified parts that exhibited similar sampled characteristics. Total quality management and concurrent engineering have been demonstrated to produce improved reliability with lower costs on recent tactical nozzle programs. Concurrent engineering involving the customer, designers, vendors, manufacturing and quality personnel should be practiced even during prototype or demonstrated nozzle development. Examples of some nondestructive and destructive tests used on typical tactical nozzle programs for raw material, component, or metals evaluation and acceptance are summarized on Table 7 and discussed as follows:

The state of the same

# 1. Insulators or Ablative Raw Materials

Insulators or ablative raw materials, such as reinforced phenolics, are usually tested for resin content and resin flow to ensure quality components can be manufactured with established process parameters. Sometimes cured panels are tested

for density, residual volatiles, resin content, tensile or compressive strength, interlaminar shear strength, flexural strength, or acetone extractibles. Tests required may vary depending on type of raw material, vendor, and program requirements and previously have been conducted per standard American Society for Testing and Materials (ASTM) or equivalent test methods.

### Components

Components are subjected to x-ray, alcohol wipe, and tag-end testing. Tag-end tests consist of density, resin, and volatile content, acetone extractible, tensile/compressive/flexural strength, interlaminar shear, and hardness tests as noted in Table 7. Occasionally high-rate-production programs will select one component out of 10 or 20 and conduct a destruct test for lot acceptance. Defect criteria for ablative components does not usually allow cracks or delaminations. Sometimes minor delaminations and voids in qualified sizes are allowed depending on location in the component.

### Throat Materials

Graphites and carbon-carbon materials are generally subjected to 100% x-ray and alcohol wipe inspection, as well as tag-end tests for density, tensile and/or compressive strength tests as noted in Table 7. Cracks in throat materials are not acceptable, whereas low-density regions and voids are usually limited in size and quantity depending on location within the component relative to critical stress regions or expected eroded surfaces.

### . Elastomeric Insulators

Elastomeric Insulators are usually x-ray and hardness checked for inclusions, delaminations, or voids. Some inclusions or voids can be tolerated depending on size and location, whereas separations are generally considered undesirable.

## . Interfaces or Bondlines

Interfaces or Bondlines are x-ray and alcohol wipe inspected where possible or meaningful to check for voids or unbonds. Continuous unbonds that might allow gas flow to critical structural components are unacceptable, whereas intermittent unbond indications or voids can be tolerated, as long as they are qualified during nozzle development and qualification phases of a program.

# Destructive Sample Testing

Destructive sample testing is usually done by one of three methods: 1) random selection and destructive testing of one actual component of every so many produced, 10–20 being typical; 2) destructive testing of an excess part of the component or tag end; or 3) destructive testing of an excess part of the component or tag end; or 3) destructive testing of an extra test slab of the same material that has been processed with a batch of components hydroclaved with them, or heat treated with them, for example. Method 1 has the advantage that an actual part is tested but the disadvantages are the expense and the statistical possibility that a part not tested is bad. Method 2 has the advantage that a test is conducted for each part, but it is often questioned whether the tag end is truly representative of the part. Method 3 is likewise questionable as to whether the test slab is truly representative. Method 1, being the least expensive, is practical only in production after development and qualification testing has been completed and statistical limits have been

established. Methods 2 and 3 are normally used in concept demonstration and during development and qualification phases of a program. If safety factors are large enough, destructive testing is often not included in test and development programs.

### IV. Design Sequence

Because of the wide operating requirements and diversity of materials utilized for tactical nozzles, a specific design usually requires support from numerous disciplines requiring their own unique expertise. Typically a design requires involvement by persons with experience in 1) materials and processing (reference Sec. III); 2) aerodynamic design and analyses (both internal and external), 3) structural analysis of conventional and specialized metals, as well as ablative composite or high-temperature metals; 4) manufacturing knowledge of metallics and composites; and 5) reliability.

Armed with a working knowledge of the listed disciplines, as well as previous experience, a designer can prepare a preliminary design that can be used for experimental evaluation or submitted to the necessary disciplines for detailed analyses. There are no industry standard practices other than those suggested in Ref. 1 Approaches and methods vary with each company based on corporate experience.

Approaches and methods vary with each company based on corporate experience. This section will not attempt to cover details necessary for all materials and processes available, nor complete internal and external aerodynamic and structural analyses, nor all the various methods required to machine nozzle components and machines available, nor all methods available to assess a nozzle design's expected reliability, but will present a systematic step through a preliminary design. As each particular discipline is discussed, typical industry standard methods or programs used for detailed analyses, etc. will be referenced.

Designing a tactical nozzle involves three to five basic steps or processes as

Designing a tactical nozzle involves three to five basic steps or processes as outlined on Fig. 15. Detailed thermal and structural analyses in step 5 will only introduce the reader to typical analytical methods used in the industry and their reference sources.

### A. Preliminary Design

# 1. Step 1: Defining Preliminary Design Requirements

Typically a designer prepares a summary of basic or design driver requirements, such as those noted in Table 8 before starting the design sequence. From these requirements, the designer then will know what type of nozzle design, i.e., fixed with or without TVC, movable TVC, external, submerged, and so forth, is required, or whether the design requires a blast tube.

# 2. Step 2: Defining the Internal Aerodynamic Profile

This is when the designer prepares the aerodynamic profile of the nozzle inlet throat, forward exit cone region, and finally the supersonic exit cone portion.

### Nozzle Inlets

Fixed external. To provide smooth gas flow into the throat, a blend radius of one to two times the throat radius has been used. With an external nozzle design, such as that shown in Fig. 1A, this radius is joined tangentially to the conic...................................

Submerged fixed or movable. Submerged, fixed or movable nozzle inlets use the same 1.0R, blend radius immediately upstream of the throat combined with a longer entrance contour consisting of a 3:2 ellipse or hyperbolic spiral. The length of this inlet is usually one to 1.7 times the throat radius for smooth gas flow transition. The radius at the nose tip typically is 1.3–1.7 times the throat radius and incorporates another blend radius into the elliptical or hyperbolic portion with a small radius.

Downstream throat transition region. Smooth gas flow transition from the throat region into the supersonic exit cone is provided by a blend radius between 0.5 and 3.0 times the throat radius, depending on design envelope restrictions for

insulators and structural elements immediately aft of the throat. Occasionally, a short cylindrical section is employed in the throat of approximately 0.1-0.5 in. in length.

Exit cone. The supersonic exit cone can either be a straight conical design, usually 15–24 deg, or contoured to achieve more performance in the same or shorter length. Most tactical nozzles have expansion ratios exit area divided by the throat area of 10:1 or less because of their low-altitude operating conditions. Contoured designs can either be defined by a method of characteristics or a large radius tangent to the throat downstream blend radius and the exit plane as discussed in Ref. 4. As shown in Fig. 16, a contoured exit cone profile achieves the same diameter and expansion ratio in a shorter length.

Typical initial angles  $\theta_l$  range from 18 to 24 deg with up to 15 deg of turnback (i.e.,  $\theta_1 - \alpha$ ) demonstrated. Contour turnback is determined by exit cone liner

![](_page_197_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 16 Nozzle exit cone design parameters.

aterial selection and acceptable erosion with the particular motor propellant pe and motor operating pressure and burn times. Exit angles  $\alpha$  as low as 9 deg th an initial angle of 24 deg have been demonstrated on a performance driven tical nozzle. The preliminary aerodynamic profile has been defined and layed t as shown in Fig. 17.

# Step 3: Sizing the Inlet-Throat-Exit Cone Ablatives

With the preliminary aerodynamic profile completed, the designer may address insulative and ablative (or liner) materials, including the throat. Materials are ected for two primary reasons: their ablative resistance and thermal insulating pacity. Secondary considerations include strength and ductility (at all temperaces), density, cost, and processibility. The material selected for the throat, more an any other, is generally selected for its erosion rate. A material's erosion rate dependent on several factors, including propellant composition, flame temperare, operating pressure, flow condition (Mach number), geometry (diameter), d support. For composite materials, erosion rates, and to a lesser degree thermal nductivity, vary as a function of the materials' ply orientation to the flow surface, e general rule is that a material, oriented with its ply or grain direction less than equal to 75 deg downstream to the flow surface, will offer adequate erosion istance without creating an internal thermal-structural problem, especially with bon phenolic composites.

With all of these factors in mind, the nozzle designer must lay out a configurant hat puts materials in the proper position and orientation to ensure a relatively ooth flow surface throughout motor operation. In addition, the chosen materials ist provide sufficient insulation to maintain the integrity of the nozzle structured keep backside temperatures within requirement limits. In many cases, the terrial required to provide the desired erosion resistance does not provide sufferint insulation capability, requiring a second material with improved insulative parties outboard of the ablative.

As previously mentioned, there are other factors that must be considered, inding material cost, processibility, and reliability. Some materials such as carbon of phenolic (CCP), have a long history of use in solid rocket motor nozzles, properties are well documented and processing parameters well understood, t material prices are very high, usually \$50–100 per pound. Silica cloth phenos are lower in cost, approximately \$25–40 per pound, and have demonstrated equate erosion resistance for low and nonaluminized propellants.

![](_page_197_Figure_11.jpeg)

Step 2: Defining the Aerodynamic Contour

![](_page_197_Picture_13.jpeg)

![](_page_197_Picture_14.jpeg)

Fig. 17 Preliminary nozzle design sequence.

Phenolic components are typically fabricated using one of three methods: 1) tape wrapped on a mandrel using straight or ±45-deg bias tape, 2) compression molded using precut patterns, or 3) compression molded using chopped squares or macerated fibers. The first two methods, which are the most labor intensive and costly, yield a component with known and repeatable ply orientations optimized for material strength and erosion resistance. In contrast, the third method yields a more random, less repeatable orientation. Thus, the use

of costly materials or fabrication techniques must only be selected after carefully considering all nozzle requirements and design goals. In a nutshell, the material selection design trade involves selection of a material that will fulfill all design requirements and be consistent with the objectives of any particular design.

# Throat Material Selection and Design

The motor ballistician usually defines a maximum allowable throat erosion rate (i.e., material loss rate) that can be tolerated without adversely affecting the desired motor ballistics. Usually this rate limits the throat area increase to less than 5% and 10% is considered excessive as noted by Wong.

Polycrystalline (or bulk) graphites have been used as throat materials in highly aluminized and nonaluminized propellants up to  $6500^{\circ}F$  exhaust gas temperatures and 1000-psi chamber pressures. Typically bulk graphites have experienced thermal shock failures early in motor burn and must possess the proper thermal stress parameters, as well as configurations, to function adequately. As suggested in Ref. 4, a combination of thermal conductivity K times tensile strength S divided by the multiplication of modulus of elasticity E times the coefficient of thermal expansion  $\alpha$  should be greater than  $20 \times 10^3$  Btu/ft-h or

$$K \cdot S/E\alpha \ge 20 \times 10^3$$
 Btu/ft-h

Usually square shaped cross sections are utilized as much as possible for bulk graphite components.

Pyrolytic graphite and tungsten alloys have also been used often in tactical nozzle throats, all requiring some sort of thermal expansion allowances. Thermal expansion provisions have been provided by washers of polytetrafluorethylene, silicone rubber, adhesives, or Grafoil materials. The most widely used approach has been thin washers of polytetrafluorethylene strategically placed for axial thermal growth and properly selected adhesive bond gaps on the outer surfaces between the graphite, pyrolytic graphite, or tungsten component and the insulator. A rule of thumb for sizing these thermal expansion elements is as follows:

a) Bulk graphite backside bond use gaps of 0.4% of the radius with carbon phenolics, and 0.4% R minus 0.005 in. with silica phenolics. Axial growth allowances should be approximately 10% of axial thicknesses.

b) Pyrolytic graphite backside bond use gaps of 0.002-0.006 in. and approximately 80% of the axial free thermal expansion.

c) Axial spacers of polytetrafluorethylene are typically 0.06 in./inch of axial thickness for pyrolytic graphite; 0.01 in./inch of bulk graphites; and 0.015 in./inch of carbon-carbons.

Recently, tactical motors utilized reduced or nonaluminized propellants that allow the use of noncarbonaceous silica or glass phenolic composites to be used in the throat region, as well as the inlet and exit cone areas.

Predicting the material loss rate or erosion rate can be an extensive procedure either using Aerotherm's ACE computer program<sup>7</sup> or scaled from rates measured for the same throat material from similar nozzle tests with similar propellants. Most often the designer scales a throat erosion rate using a simplified equation involving throat diameters and pressures (from Ref. 4) as

$$\dot{e}_r = \dot{e}_r \text{ known} \left( \frac{P \text{ new}}{P \text{ known}} \right)^{0.8} \left( \frac{D \text{ throat known}}{D \text{ throat new}} \right)^{0.2}$$

This equation can be used directly for silica phenolics without any corrections for chemical composition of the propellant exhaust products. Correction for carbonaceous materials needs a factor that is too extensive to cover here, but a more detailed discussion can be found in Ref. 8.

Char, or in-depth thermal profiles, can be determined either from Aerotherm's charring material and ablation (CMA) computer program (Ref. 8) or scaled from similar nozzle test results that used the same materials in similar propellant environment. A simplified scaling equation uses char depth as a function of burn time and exhaust gas products flame temperature, or

$$\begin{aligned} \text{char depth}_{\text{desired}} &= \text{char depth}_{\text{known}} \left( \frac{\text{burntime} - \text{new}}{\text{burntime known}} \right)^X \\ &\times \left( \frac{\text{new flame temperature}}{\text{flame temperature} - \text{known}} \right)^Y \end{aligned}$$

where X and Y coefficients are empirically derived from motor test history. This scaling equation is valid for the throat and inlet regions where the chamber exhaust gas temperatures are fairly constant and radiative heating is a strong factor. Results in the exit cone at comparable expansion ratios may be slightly conservative due to decreasing gas temperatures.

# Step 4: Structural Components Sizing

Designing the nozzle structures requires definition of the loads and the different combinations that are possible. The primary sources are internal gas pressures and thermal expansion of the liner components. Secondary loads from TVC nechanisms, external aerodynamic fin-reaction loads, and vibration-induced loads also need to be evaluated.

Structural designs are selected for specific applications considering the magnitude of the load, weight limitations, if any, and the complexity of the nozzle configuration.

Preliminary sizing of structural components can be done using simple standards of strength of materials equations and procedures such as those defined by Roark. Definition of metals, their capabilities, and recommended uses can be found in the latest edition of MIL-Handbook-5E. Most structural components are sized to minimize deflections of insulator components.

# 7. Step 5: Final Nozzle Design and Detailed Analyses

With the preliminary liner and structures sized, they are combined with the preliminary aerodynamic profiles defined in step 2 and compared to interface envelope requirements defined in step 1 and weighed if required. Adjustments may be necessary to smoothly blend and interface as required.

Once the basic layout and materials have been selected, the designer must review how various components are assembled. High-strength, structural adhesives with excellent high-temperature capability are often used to bond insulating components to the structural shell and to each other. If backside temperatures become too hot during operation, a good insulating material will be incorporated outboard of the ablatives, sometimes tape wrapped directly over the ablative and cured in place. In some cases, positive mechanical retention is added to ensure that the

liner materials are not expelled. This is accomplished by incorporating a mechanical stop into the nozzle structure or using metallic or plastic pins that penetrate through the structure directly into the plastic liner.

As the nozzle plastic materials are heated, they expand, and if this expansion is constrained, large thermal stresses can develop. Thus, expansion relief must be incorporated into the design between components to minimize this effect. Adhesive bond gaps or materials such as polytetrafluorethylene, polypropylene, or foam that burns out shortly after motor ignition are used to provide the desired expansion gaps.

Pyrolyzing materials pose another problem that must be considered in the design layout. As the materials char, they go through a pyrolysis process that generates a significant amount of gases or off gassing. This off gassing must be allowed to vent or significant forces may be generated that lead to local material failures in the form of spalling or excessive erosion. Venting is accomplished through the mentioned gaps and/or orienting the material with ply directions that allow these gasses to be vented between the laminates as discussed earlier in step 3 of this section.

## . Detailed Analyses

### Aerodynamic Analysis

The objective of the aerodynamic design phase is to optimize the nozzle contour (inlet and exit) within the motor/nozzle constraints to achieve optimum nozzle performance or maximum  $I_{sp}$ . This is accomplished analytically using the industry standard program, SPP.<sup>6</sup>

Basically, SPP calculates the maximum or ideal theoretical performance for a rocket motor and then degrades it by subtracting individual calculated losses that are known to occur within the motor and nozzle. The assumption that all losses can be treated independently from each other has been substantiated throughout the industry. The only constraint is that for any given number of individual losses defined, each physical source of nonideal performance loss is accounted for once and only once. A description of the SPP program and its modules is presented in Fig. 18, as well as a description of the various performance losses calculated and their typical ranges of values. Although there are several variables that control the final nozzle contour, experience has identified the following parameters as the most influential on overall nozzle performance (see description shown earlier in Fig. 16: exit cone length (L) in relation to throat radius ( $R_T$ ), ( $L/R_T$ ); expansion ratio ( $A_e/A_t$ ) or  $A/A^*$ ; exit cone initial (or entrance) angle  $\Theta_i$ ; exit cone exit angle (a); and exit cone initial blend radius in relationship to the throat radius ( $R_e/R_T$ ).

## Thermal Analysis

Thermal analysis determines surface recession or erosion rates as well as indepth thermal response of the nozzle ablatives and insulators. Erosion rates can be affected by mechanical as well as thermal failure mechanisms depending on component and material configurations and flow conditions. Thus, experienced analysts depend heavily on past experience to choose the correct modeling technique for any given combination of propellant, operating conditions, material, and component geometries.

![](_page_199_Figure_13.jpeg)

Fig. 18 Solid propellant rocket motor performance. Prediction computer program (SSP) summary.

There are several computer codes available to predict thermal responses in nozzles. A flow diagram for a typical aerothermal analysis is presented in Fig. 19. A description of the computer codes used for the required input and output of each is presented in Table 9. This section presents an overview of the basic principals involved thereby establishing a general understanding of the ablation/heat transfer solution methodology.

Thermal analyses consists of defining heat transfer boundary conditions that are input to oneor two-dimensional axisymmetric analytic codes that predict surface codes in the description of the Recovery enthalpy is the driving potential for convective heat transfer. This parameter is calculated using local static enthalpy and Prandtl numbers obtained from Aerotherm's Chemical Equilibrium (ACE) program by

$$h_r = h_s + Pr^{\frac{1}{3}}(h_c - h_s)$$

Fig. 19 Thermal analysis methodology.

where

= chamber enthalpy, Btu/lbm

 $h_c = \text{chamber enthalpy, Btu/lbm}$ Pr = local Prandtl number

Convective heat transfer coefficients are calculated. The convective heat transfer coefficients are calculated from the SHARP flow solution using the Reynolds analogy, in which the Stanton number St is related to the skin friction coefficient  $C_f$  as follows:

$$St = C_f P_r^{.4/3}$$

where Pr is the laminar Prandtl number and  $C_f$  is related to the wall shear stress  $\tau_{\omega}$ , density  $\rho$ , and the parallel to wall velocity u as follows:

$$C_f = \tau_w/\rho |u|^2$$

where u is assumed to have either a parabolic (laminer sublayer) or a logarithmic law of the wall profile depending on the local Reynolds number.

These local heat transfer coefficients are then combined with the enthalpies predicted by the ACE program, <sup>7</sup> and the resulting values can be input either to the one-dimensional charring materials and ablation (CMA) or two-dimensional axisymmetric transient heating and material ablation (ASCHAR) computer programs for erosion, char, and in-depth thermal profile definition.

Thermal analyses. The CMA and ASCHAR computer codes handle a very complex physical phenomena schematically represented in Fig. 20. Carbon phenolic and carbon–carbon materials are modeled as diffusion controlled equilibrium reactions directly affected by the rates of mass transfer to heat transfer Stanton numbers. Elastomeric materials behave in a similar mode. Silica phenolic

![](_page_201_Figure_14.jpeg)

Fig. 20 Aerothermal analysis, general physical problem.

materials are modeled using a fail temperature, which melts the material away. High-temperature refractory metals, such as tungsten and its alloys, are modeled with surface failure temperature or melting erosion mode. The ratio of mass transfer to heat transfer can be approximated by the following equation.

$$Cm/Ch = (Pr/Sc)^{2/3}$$

where

Cm = mass transfer Stanton number

Ch = heat transfer Stanton number

Pr = Prandtl number

Sc = Schmidt number

Each material is modeled with a set of temperature dependent properties that include the following: density (initial and charred), specific heat, thermal conductivity (in-plane and across-ply), emissivity, heat of formation, and chemical decomposition data.

9 4 ...................................

Output. The results of the aerothermal analyses provide a description of the eroded nozzle surface, progression of the char layer, and in-depth temperatures through the thickness of the nozzle for any time during motor operation. The in-depth temperatures are used in subsequent structural analyses to determine the thermal stress state in the nozzle components at any given time. This will discussed further in the following section. Heat transfer analysis is also conducted to determine the maximum temperature of the nozzle structure at the conclusion of heat soak.

The aerothermal analysis provides an indication of the erosion and thermal conditions in the nozzle. Several iterations may be required utilizing data obtained from actual nozzle testing such as backside temperatures from thermocouples and posttest measurements of the char depth and eroded contours. This database is continually updated and conducted to improve the material and thermochemical models.

### Structural Analyses

Structural analysis utilizes data and information from all the preceding sections to evaluate the structural integrity of the nozzle assembly. This section provides a synopsis of the required inputs (loads and boundary conditions), material models required, and structural mechanics models available for use.

Overall structural analyses, described in Fig. 21, consists of 1) evaluating the primary metallic or composite structures that operate within well-defined temperature-dependent materials properties ranges, such as those defined in handbooks, 9,10 and 2) thermal-structural evaluation of ablative or insulator composites that operate under high temperatures and temperature gradients. These two tasks are briefly discussed in the following sections.

Primary structures analysis. Evaluation of most tactical nozzle primary structures, usually composed of high-strength stainless or alloy steel, is a relatively straightforward problem involving standard strength of materials calculations. Linear elastic material models are generally used with standard failure criteria such as ultimate strength or Von Mises equivalent stresses. The structure is often analyzed separately from the nozzle insulating components to allow for finer mesh concentration in critical structural areas, incorporation of asymmetric loads

7

NOZZLE DESIGN

![](_page_202_Figure_4.jpeg)

or geometry, and possibly even nonlinear effects. The insulating components are often simulated in these models as a low modulus, isotropic "mush" material to allow the correct pressure loading distribution to be applied to the structure. This method allows for the use of more standard codes such as ABAQUS' in evaluating the structure, without concern for the orthotropic insulating materials and their complex, thermally induced stress states.

Ablatives/insulator thermostructural analyses. Typical ablative/insulator composite thermostructural analysis is conducted using finite element (FE) analytical techniques. At present, there are no industry standardized codes. General procedures are fairly common, however, and company proprietary finite element codes are similar in many ways. Examples of such codes are listed in Refs. 11–14. The NASA Solid Propulsion Integrity Program (SPIP) is currently attempting to de-

Analyzing ablative and insulator composites is much more difficult due to erosion, resin decomposition, and in some cases extreme temperature gradients throughout their cross-sectional configuration during motor operation. Temperatures within an ablative component may vary from over 5000°F at the inner-gas surface to -65°F at the outer structural interface. Temperature gradients may be as high as 2000°F difference per each 1/10 in. of thickness. Heating rates in excess of 1000°F/s at the gas surface are also common.

thermostructural analysis. This code will probably not be available until the late

velop an industry standardized code specifically for nozzle insulator and ablatives

Ablative and insulator composites are anisotropic and nonhomogenous because of their combination of resin and/or fibrous or woven reinforcements. Sometimes composites exhibit nonuniform strength and modulus properties in different directions that also vary with temperature, heating rate, loads, and pressure levels.
