Asymmetric loads may be introduced through structural components to the ablative insulators due to TVC loads associated with a movable nozzle. Dynamic loads due to accelerations or vibrations are handled by superimposing a dynamic load factor onto the appropriate static load or stress. These dynamic load factors are derived from separate dynamic analyses.

The general shape of rocket nozzles lend themselves nicely to two-dimensional finite element axisymmetric models. Typical input consists of a description of geometry defined by a finite element grid, material, properties, boundary conditions, thermal environment, and pressure distribution as discussed later. Analysis output is typically expressed in terms of elemental stresses, strains, and nodal displacements that are compared to material allowable strength, using suitable failure criteria, to determine adequacy.

The nozzle geometry is represented by a finite element grid. Care must be taken to utilize minimum structural material thicknesses and account for all material interfaces and bondlines. The FE grid used for a typical tactical nozzle exit cone is shown in Fig. 22. Throughout motor operation, the nozzle geometry changes as the materials near the flow surface erode. Most nozzle codes automatically adjust for this and apply appropriate pressure loads throughout motor operation based on input from the thermal model. Structural and thermal analysis finite element models need to be coordinated for efficient transfer of thermal gradients and eroded surface conditions.

Boundary conditions are applied to the FE model to simulate the nozzle support and mechanical loads. Interfaces and joints are simulated to transmit load into the correct direction (axial, radial, etc.). The motor pressure load is applied to

![](_page_203_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 22 Typical exit cone assembly thermostructural analysis finite element grid.

the internal contour of the nozzle and adjusted along the length of the nozzle to simulate the pressure distribution resulting from the various flow conditions along the nozzle. This distribution is typically obtained directly from the results of the aerodynamic analysis or may be estimated using standard one-dimensional isentropic flow tables. Pressure levels may need to be adjusted throughout burntime to match motor variations.

Nozzle material properties are generally obtained from databases generated by individual companies or agencies such as NASA, the Air Force, or the Navy. It takes a great deal of time and money to fully characterize a material to obtain statistically meaningful temperature-dependent structural properties, including those at elevated temperatures suitable for use in analytical models.

Most ablative or insulator materials are orthotropic, with properties dependent on the orientation of the material layup or grain direction. Some are more random, as is the case for molding compounds. Phenolic ablative composites exhibit linear-elastic temperature-dependent properties up to 500–700°F, but nonlinear behavior is exhibited above this temperature range. Metals exhibit linear-elastic properties below their yield strength, and nonlinear behavior above, as discussed in MIL-Handbook-5E.

Carbon—carbon materials, because of their multidirectional reinforcing construction exhibit multimodulus materials properties as a function of temperature. Sometimes a Jones—Nelson multimodulus materials properties model is used for these materials.

Typical ablative composite materials properties models consist of modulus, Poisson's ratios, and shear modulii as a function of temperature. Bondlines are modeled to transmit compressive and shear loads across them to simulate the correct degree of material interaction. As the temperature increases, the material properties are degraded accordingly.

Thermal environment inputs are obtained directly from the results of the thermal analyses. The eroded depth is simulated and in-depth temperature responses are applied as elemental temperatures. These thermal gradients, such as those mentioned earlier in Section 3, and material properties are used to calculated the thermal stress field of the finite element model. This input is available for any distinct time during motor operation using time increments down to 0.1 s. Structural analysis is generally conducted at discrete time intervals to identify the maximum stress state for each component. These generally include but are not limited to the following: 0-2.0 s ignition transient, maximum thermal gradient

inducing thermal shock; time when pressure is maximum; end of burn when maximum material degradation has occurred and pressure has decayed to ambient. The term thermal shock refers to the large tensile stress field that develops due to the steep thermal gradient through the component thickness. This is especially critical for brittle materials, such as tungsten and graphites. At motor ignition, the inner diameter of materials exposed to the flame front heats very rapidly almost instantaneously, while the outer portion of the material remains cool. As the inner material tries to expand, the growth is restrained by the outer material resulting in potentially very large tensile stresses in the middle of the part. The thicker the cross-sectional area, the more susceptible these materials are to thermal shock.

For output the finite element programs calculate elemental stresses and strains in the principal directions of each material and at all locations in the model. These are compared to the capabilities of the materials at the representative temperature to calculate a structural margin of safety. The standard failure criterion is ultimate material strength, at temperature, in all principal material directions. The non-linear nature of some materials, however, requires the use of an ultimate strain criterion. Other criteria have been developed for composite materials that are more representative of potential failure modes. The following three failure criteria have been established relative to the material(s) defined.

1) For metals uniaxial equivalent stresses using Von Mises yield theory are compared to allowables from handbooks or component data. Exceeding the yield strength locally or in regions surrounded by compressive stresses is not necessarily unacceptable. Generally acceptable metallic structural temperature limits are 450°F for steels, 250°F for aluminums, and 200°F for epoxy composites.

2) For phenolic ablative/insulator composites the maximum fiber stress criterion defined by the lamina stress state is the primary failure criteria and is compared to fiber strength allowables to assess possibility of fiber rupture in the noncharred temperature region less than 500°F. A secondary failure criteria is delamination between plies that could allow gas flow to structural components and burn through or excessive heating due to radiative heating and subsequent failure. Quadratic failure boundary criteria, such as that shown in Fig. 23, which combine interlaminar tensile or compressive strengths and interlaminar she ar strengths, have proven successful on some tactical programs.<sup>15</sup>

![](_page_203_Figure_16.jpeg)

Fig. 23 Phenolic composite quadratic failure boundary.

NOZZLE DESIGN

3) Epoxy structural adhesive bondlines are usually limited to temperatures less than or equal to 200°F because of modulus degradation. Phenolic bonded interfaces are limited to 600°F. Structural bondlines in compression are evaluated using predicted shear stresses compared to allowables, since normal compression failure would still transmit the leads or retain the abaltive or insulator components. Some bondlines are considered only for thermal expansion capability and structural retention during manufacturing and handling.

As stated earlier, the stress output and subsequent margin of safety calculations are merely tools to aid in the overall assessment of the nozzle integrity. The nature of phenolic materials and the manner in which they char and ablate are in reality a series of localized material failures. It is by controlling or limiting their local failures to the char region and assessing the integrity of the remainder of the component that determines the survivability of the nozzle assembly.

### C. Mass Properties

Sometimes the design sequence requires several iterations to achieve a nozzle design that provides adequate performance and has sufficient thermal protection and predicted positive structural margins. The nozzle mass and c.g. are then calculated and compared to required limits. The position of the nozzle at the extreme aft end of the motor has a major impact on the c.g. and subsequent performance characteristics of the missile system. Nozzle weight losses due to ablation and the change in density of the charred material are also calculated and submitted for motor performance evaluations. These weight losses are generally less than 5–12% of the original ablative/insulator weights.

### Development Testing

## Laboratory Bench Testing

As the nozzle design matures, the details are documented by engineering drawings, acceptance requirements, material specifications, and sometimes process definitions for critical process operations either by specifications or often contained directly on the engineering drawings. As prototype hardware is fabricated, the first step in the evaluation process typically involves laboratory bench tests to evaluate structural capability. Although these tests usually only duplicate pressure-induced physical loads, they are used to verify the structural margins of the key structural components and bondlines. The tests are also used to verify structural analyses by monitoring strain gauge responses during testing. The benefits of bench testing tactical-sized systems is that the tests can reasonably be run on actual-sized hardware.

Bench testing is particularly useful in evaluating movable nozzles. It is very valuable to obtain early verification of the nozzle response and maximum torque required to drive the nozzle. These early data are essential to successfully size the actuation system.

### . Static Tests

Although attempts have been made in a laboratory to simulate the extreme temperatures and heating rates nozzles are subjected to in a rocket motor, there is currently no substitute for a static test. Static tests provide the ultimate verification of the rocket motor and nozzle design. The relative inexpensiveness of

tactical-sized systems makes static tests a very cost-effective method of evaluation, especially considering the extent of data that can be obtained.

Ballisticians can verify the actual performance of the motor, calculate specific impulse, and evaluate the effect of throat erosion on motor pressure-time trace.

Strain gauges and thermocouples applied to the nozzle during static test are used to record deflections and backside temperatures during and after motor burnout. These data are used to correlate the thermal and structural models.

The nozzle is removed from the motor following static test and sectioned to allow inspection of subsurface conditions of components. Actual erosion and char lines can be measured and correlated to thermal model predictions. Components are also inspected in detail to identify any anomalous cracks or delaminations that may have developed.

## V. Thrust Vector Control

Most tactical motors require some steering to meet flight maneuvering requirements. In addition to, or replacing, traditional external aerodynamic control fins, steering control has been demonstrated by deflecting the nozzles hot exhaust gases, or the motor thrust, as shown in Fig. 24.

Both aerodynamic and mechanical techniques have been used to redirect the motor thrust and provide steering forces. This section limits itself mainly to mechanical thrust vector control systems, with a brief description of two major aerodynamic techniques, jet tabs and jet vanes. Aerodynamic techniques have demonstrated very rapid response rates, but also suffer motor thrust losses at large TVC vector angles as discussed later in Sec. V.B. The higher losses with the aerodynamic jet tab, jet vane, and bleed control concepts are a result of the physical creation of side force by creating a snock pattern in the exit cone thrust, i.e., the higher the TVC angle required, the larger the percentage of thrust involved. Movable nozzles exhibit much lower thrust losses while providing TVC, because they redirect the motor and nozzle thrust without converting dynamic energy to

This section will briefly describe the different types of TVC nozzles, compare them in tradeoffs, and present examples of typical tactical systems evaluated or used in production programs.

# A. Classification of TVC Systems

TVC systems are classified primarily by nozzle type, either fixed or movable, and second by the method of providing actual thrust vector control, such as fixed liquid injection thrust vector control (LITVC) or movable-flex bearing thrust vector control. A complete summary of all possible types of TVC systems is presented in Fig. 25.

# 1. Fixed Nozzles TVC Systems

Thrust vector control with fixed nozzles is provided by injection of a liquid, or by hot chamber gases, or by mechanically deflecting tabs or vanes in the exhaust

Secondary injection. LITVC is achieved by injecting a reactive liquid, such as nitrogen tetroxide, strontium perchlorate, hydrazine, Freon, or hydrogen peroxide, into the supersonic exit cone region. Side force is achieved from recovered

Mechanical deflectors. Jet tabs or vanes<sup>17</sup> have been used for rapid thrust vector control steering, especially early in flight when missile speeds are too low to achieve effective control with external aerodynamic fins. These systems, shown in Fig. 27, usually require, very heavy tungsten or refractory metal components and, therefore, resulting heavy, actuation systems due to the high inertias at the rapid response rates desired. These systems are usually attached aft of the exit plane as a separate package, so they can be jettisoned and not carried throughout the mission. Jet tabs are inserted in and out of the exhaust plume as needed, whereas jet vanes remain in the plume constantly and vector about their own retention shaft.

More recently, jet tab TVC has been used on one production missile, whereas jet vanes have been incorporated onto an antisubmarine rocket booster system. A more detailed discussion of jet tab and jet vane TVC systems and design can be found in Ref. 19.

# 2. Movable Nozzles TVC Systems

Movable nozzle TVC systems are generally categorized by 1) type of hot-gas sealing concept, such as flexible bearing, o-ring, fluid bearing, or bellows; 2) type of pressure blowout load retention, such as flexible bearing, gimbal, or trapped ball and socket; 3) submerged or desubmerged; and 4) location of splitline between the fixed or stationary and movable portions.

Recently the most common tactical TVC system has been a trapped ball (both submerged and desubmerged) because of their low torque and small envelope packaging ability for TVC vector angles from 10–20 deg. Fluid bearing TVC systems were demonstrated with 15–20 deg capability, but require larger envelopes and result in heavier systems than trapped ball or flex bearing concepts. Recently, improved materials and design/analytical capabilities have resulted in smaller, more compact, and low torque ±8-deg TVC flexible bearing design. Because of the complexity involved in designing and analyzing either a trapped ball or flexible bearing TVC mechanism, this section will address only basic sizing considerations for these two TVC concepts.

Subsonic splitline. Subsonic splitline designs located in a submerged region with a Mach number less than 0.10 are by for the most common type of tactical movable nozzle design. They require special design considerations to ensure propellant exhaust gases do not cause excessive heating of critical structural components or other serious problems. Designs must keep adjoining insulators from swelling and interfering with nozzle movement. A design must also prevent the splitline from filling with semimolten exhaust products that could solidify causing torques to increase or interfere with nozzle movement.

11

Subsonic splitline gap widths are typically 0.02-0.05 in. wide and in excess of 1 in. in length (approximately), depending on materials and burntimes.

Supersonic splitline. Supersonic splitline designs, such as that described in Ref. 20, contribute their own unique considerations, such as loads and splitline flow due to turning the supersonic exhaust in the exit cone. Until full flow is developed in the exit cone, there can be an instantaneous separation load between the fixed and movable sections that must be reacted by external support. Once exit cone flow is established, a net compressive load exists across the gap between the fixed and movable sections. The external support has typically been two passive (noncontrolled) actuators or gas bearing mechanisms similar to slock absorbers.

12-11-11

Trapped ball. Trapped ball TVC nozzles consist of a spherically shaped movable ball section that rides on and is supported by a mating spherically shaped fixed portion, as shown in Fig. 28. This figure also illustrates submerged and desubmerged trapped ball concepts. Movable section blowout loads are reacted by a low-friction material, typically a thin layer of polytetrafluorethylene.

Figure 29 briefly describes trapped ball TVC sizing considerations, and typical design capabilities. As noted, the socket is usually designed to minimize deflections so that a true spherical surface is maintained for the low liner friction material. As the contact angle decreases, the point bearing load increases due to decreased projected axial bearing area. Most trapped ball designs have demonstrated contact angles between 18 and 41 deg. Thin 0.015-in-thick, polytetrafluorethylene materials have demonstrated contact pressures from 12,000–25,000 psi. Friction coefficients decrease as bearing pressures increase. Because movement and friction generate heat, nozzle actuation torques also decrease with increasing temperatures up to a point where bondlines and polytetrafluorethylene materials begin to

![](_page_207_Figure_5.jpeg)

Fig. 30 Types of movable TVC nozzles.

physically degrade. Torques increase with velocity mainly due to inertial effects, not friction coefficients. Specific designs depend on size, materials, and design details too numerous to cover here. Friction coefficients range from 0.015–0.025 for breakout starting motion from rest to 0.01–0.015 during operation. Trapped ball TVC concepts can be designed with several different splittine and pivot point locations as shown in Fig. 30 for tailored TVC system depending on system and envelope requirements. Vector angles as high as 35 deg have been demonstrated on a trapped ball TVC system used for a crew ejection system.

Flex bearing. Flex bearing TVC systems that are used almost exclusively on larger strategic and very large commercial launch vehicle nozzles have recently been pursued for use on tactical systems requiring TVC angles of 5–15 deg. Flex bearings consist of alternating layers of spherically shaped reinforcements and elastomer pads vulcanized together and to the forward and aft end rings. Thrust vector is achieved by shear deformation of the elastomer pads. Reinforcements vector is achieved by shear deformation of the elastomer pads. Reinforcements vectors, natural compounds, as well as synthetic polysisoprenes. Silicones exhibit cones, natural compounds, as well as synthetic polysisoprenes. Silicones exhibit very little modulus change over typical tactical motor operating temperature ranges from –65 to +165°F. Natural rubber compounds exhibit low shear modulus values at moderate temperatures from +45 to +145°F, but cannot be used at temperatures below 20°F. Polyisoprenes are good for temperatures as low as –25°F, but not above +145°F. Flex bearing torques increase with vector angle, pressure, and age, as shown in Fig. 31. Aging effects on silicone flex bearing torque are nearly zero.

## . TVC System Tradeoffs

Generalized pros and cons of each TVC system are compared in Table 10. In addition to the specific system requirements addressed in Secs. II.A and VI.A, special considerations that need to be considered are now discussed.

Axial thrust losses occur anytime a nozzle is vectored from the missile centerline. A subsonic movable nozzle has the lowest demonstrated thrust losses with those limited to the geometric cosine function of the vector angle. The other

![](_page_208_Figure_4.jpeg)

Fig. 31 Typical flex bearing TVC design sizing parameters.

extreme is jet tabs and vanes, with resulting large impingement shock losses. Figure 32 compares thrust losses for the various systems as a function of TVC vector angle.

Roll control cannot be achieved with a single movable nozzle but requires two to four. Omnidirectional trapped ball nozzles must incorporate some sort of antirotation feature to keep the movable portion of the nozzle from spinning when actuated. Flex bearings do not typically require TVC antirotation devices.

Another factor that must be considered, and probably the most important, is demonstrated reliability. TVC has been around since the early days of rocketry, as demonstrated by Goddard's crude jet vanes in the rocket exhaust nearly 60 years ago. In the 1990s, however, only two tactical TVC systems have high demonstrated reliabilities with low performance losses: subsonic splitline cold trapped ball and flex bearings. Thus, as new designs are conceived for future missions, it is the demonstrated success of the subsonic trapped ball nozzle that is the current design of choice.

Jet vanes first used by the Germans on the V-2 rocket continued to be used exclusively after World War II on several operational and experimental tactical missile systems. In the 1960s, jet vane TVC was replaced by movable gimballed nozzles or jet tab fixed nozzle TVC that was developed in the 1970s. Jet vane and jet tab TVC systems remain strong candidates for future systems if the performance losses are tolerable. As flex bearings exhibit more demonstrated history on tactical-size nozzles, they have the associated reliability history of being demonstrated on over 3700 TVC systems without any failures.

Any of the aforementioned systems are viable for future tactical TVC missiles and like many aspects of a TVC nozzle design, the best choice is mission and systems requirements dependent.

### VI. Summary

It should be apparent that the design of a tactical solid rocket motor nozzle is a highly iterative procedure involving many distinct steps. A successful design is one that uses the information derived from each step of the process, continually updating previous data, and arriving at a fully integrated design. Analytical

### I. Introduction

ACTICAL missiles vary considerably in mass, size, shape, and purpose. For example, some are light enough to be readily carried by one person whereas others are more massive requiring large custom-built transporting, storing, and launching facilities; some are high aspect ratio darts whereas others are only slightly greater in length than diameter; some are used at close range for

rights reserved. Copyright © 1995 by the American Institute of Aeronautics and Astronautics, Inc. All

<sup>&</sup>lt;sup>1</sup>Chief Engineer, Rocket Motors Division. \*Manager of Materials, Rocket Motors Division.

line-of-sight targets whereas others are guided well beyond the horizon. It follows that in nearly all instances each tactical missile has to be individually designed.

A tactical missile consists of three basic units: the warhead, the guidance system, and the rocket motor propulsion unit. This chapter deals with a subassembly of the propulsion unit known as the rocket motor case and is intended to draw attention to the various aspects of design, materials, and fabrication techniques which need to be considered in each individual instance.

Basically, the rocket motor case has to provide a leak-free chamber that will adequately contain the stresses imposed by the high-pressure gases evolved during the burning of the propellant charge. Such chambers usually have, or at least closely approach, those structural properties that are required for the missile case; hence, it is not unusual for the motor case to be integral with the missile. However, the motor case can also be discrete, not forming part of the missile's load bearing structure, either being housed within the missile or, alternatively, being attached to the rear (in tandem) or the side (wrap round) of the missile and having the capability of being jettisoned during flight when burning is complete.

A rocket motor case can, therefore, be defined as a leak-free structural chamber of a size and shape determined by the missile requirements including a nozzle and igniter and having provision for attaching the fore and aft missile subassemblies, the latter possibly requiring a transfer or blast tube to duct hot gases past the guidance system. It must withstand the pressures and high temperatures generated by the burning propellant charge, the loads imposed by flight maneuvering, the loads imposed by propellant change of shape when stored at various temperatures, the effect of external aerodynamic heating during air carriage and/or missile flight and the effects of mechanical shock, vibration, and corrosion during storage and handling. Provision must be made for efficient and practical internal assembly and sometimes for possible maintenance dismantling.

Above all, the designer recognizes that the mass of the case is a penalty which reduces the performance of the missile and, therefore, has to be kept to a minimum; this aspect greatly influences choice of case materials and manufacturing techniques. During the design phase a number of possible solutions may be apparent and many factors need consideration before the design of an individual motor case can be finalized. Consequently, it is only possible to give concepts and guidelines and not precise design details. The process of optimization involves consideration of material properties, methods of manufacture, inspection, and proof, as well as interactions with other rocket motor and missile components.

In the three sections that follow, design, materials, and fabrication are considered separately, each section is subdivided into structural and thermal aspects. Mention is made of end closures and blast tubes; nozzles are considered in Chapter 6. A relatively recent requirement is that of insensitive munitions, and the importance of low vulnerability to fuel fire and fragment attack is discussed in this context. The need for development and qualification phases to follow the design phase and for quality assurance procedures to be incorporated throughout are highlighted. Materials and techniques mentioned are all considered to be within the limits of good engineering practice and, with proper precautions, safe to handle. Other techniques of doubtful safety, such as welding to or winding over cases already filled with live propellant, have been omitted.

In the fourth and final section a specific rocket motor case design is given as an illustration of the steps to be undertaken during design.

## II. Design Considerations

### A. Performance

Consideration of environmental conditions will be influenced by the application; land use often will necessitate wide temperature extremes, particularly for solar radiation, and air use will require more stringent vibration environments. Interfaces with the missile must be considered, such as attachment points, fittings for wings, launch attachments, and cable conduits; all affect performance and their effects need minimizing during the design and development stages of the motor. Performance as an insensitive munition, if specified, must be considered as an integral part of the motor design and may also affect the available impulse.

### B. Structural

### General

For design purposes the motor case may be divided into a number of components, as shown in Fig. 1, each of which reacts with adjacent components. The body tube carries the main loading comprising internal pressure stresses, missile bending stresses, and localized stresses from wing and launcher attachments. The end rings provide attachment points for the end closures and interface points with the missile components. The end closures complete the pressure vessel and may provide an igniter attachment boss at the forward end and a nozzle attachment boss, with or without an intervening blast tube, at the aft end.

Not surprisingly many of these components are made from readily available engineering materials, usually good quality steels and aluminum alloys and occasionally ittanium alloys. These are homogeneous materials exhibiting isotropic properties and they are the subject of Secs. II.B.2–6. Scaling of joints between components is discussed in Sec. II.B.7. In addition, other types of construction are available which make use of higher specific strength materials in which, in some

![](_page_212_Picture_4.jpeg)

instances, the structural properties of the components are anisotropic. Design with these materials is discussed in Sec. II.B.8.

# Body Tube: Pressure Considerations

Factors that have to be taken into account when designing the body tube component are internal pressure, motor and missile stiffness requirements, attachments and their loading, and, for certain missiles, external pressure. Of these, the overriding consideration is usually the internal pressure which is associated with the gas temperatures and velocities and the burn time of the propellant charge. All of these may vary considerably from design to design and may, for example, cover the ranges 20–250° x 10° Nm<sup>-2</sup> pressure, 2500–3600 K temperature, 0–2500 ms<sup>-1</sup> velocity, and 1–60 s time. In general, however, the structural material is insulated from the hot gases in order to limit its temperature rise, and this ensures its room temperature properties are virtually unaffected, thus allowing the initial design to be carried out on the structure based solely on the internal pressure and ignoring gas temperature, gas velocity, and burn time, and utilizing the well-defined room temperature properties of the chosen materials. Occasionally, however, unitasulated structural materials are used and, in these instances, heat transfer calculations have to be carried out and the structural properties evaluated at high temperatures.

The maximum pressure expected during the motor firing, the limit load, will be supplied by the charge designer. It is typically defined in the U.K. as that pressure that will not be exceeded on more than 1 firing in 1000 with 95% confidence at the upper firing temperature. At the initial design stage an allowance for statistical variation will have been included in the limit load. In established designs this load will be accurately known from firing records. The limit load is multiplied by a proof factor of 1.125, and this results in a pressure at which all cases will subsequently be proof-pressure tested. Where case failure would endanger personnel or launch apparatus, as is usual, a safety factor of 1.33 is imposed on the limit load to provide the designer with a minimum burst level for the case design. To minimize mass it is necessary to work as closely as possible to the safety factored value. Definitions

and safety factors vary slightly from country to country; the preceding comments refer to those factors currently applied in the U.K.

SOLID ROCKLY CASE DESIGN

refer to those factors currently applied in the U.K. For an initial estimate of tube thickness, based on internal pressure, the simple formula for hoop stress in a thin-walled pressure vessel is used, Fig. 2a, hoop stress is equal to load/area, i.e.,

![](_page_212_Figure_11.jpeg)

Fig. 2 Stress considerations.

In all instances the weakest structure is considered, that is, the thickness is that at the minimum end of the thickness tolerance band and the material strength is the minimum specified. The resulting thickness may give a first indication of the material that should be considered. For example, a small diameter or a low-pressure motor may require steel that is too thin to be handleable thus leading to consideration of aluminum alloy.

It should be noted that the longitudinal stress in the pressure vessel is only half the hoop stress, Fig. 2b, longitudinal stress is equal to load/area, i.e.,

$$S_L = \frac{p\pi d^2}{4\pi dt'}$$
$$= \frac{pd}{4t'}$$

Hence, all pressure vessel structures fabricated from isotropic materials from a design that accommodates the hoop stress will have double the required capacity in the longitudinal direction. Isotropic materials subjected to bidirectional loading will achieve a higher ultimate hoop stress than if they were unidirectionally hoop loaded because of the negative strain induced by the longitudinal load, Fig. 2c.

The thickness determined for the body is usually consistent with that described as a thin wall structure. Occasionally this is not the case and the appropriate formulas then have to be applied.

For short length-to-diameter bodies, theories suggest that the thickened end sections interact to reduce the maximum hoop stress, thus allowing thinner sections to be used. However, for bodies with length-to-diameter ratios as low as 1:1, practical testing, including burst tests, does not indicate any significant effect from the end rings.

# . Body Tube: Stiffness Considerations

It is quite usual to find that the thickness of the material chosen based on pressure considerations will also be sufficient to cope with handling and flight maneuvering loads which are generally stiffness related. However, care has to be taken to analyze this aspect at the design stage and, in this respect, finite element stress analysis is a powerful aid used extensively in motor case design for evaluating stresses and strains allowing the design of more optimized components which could require less testing. The structure or continuum to be analyzed is modeled as a set of elements of finite size connected only at points called nodes. Each node has specified freedoms of translation and rotation in response to the neighboring elements or boundary conditions. The stresses are obtained from the field of nodal displacements together with the appropriate constitutive equations for the material properties.

Significant advances are still being made in the basic computer stress analysis programs, some programs being very general whereas others have been developed for a specific industry, but however sophisticated these programs, in general they can only analyze a structure; they are design aids and cannot replace designers and stress analysts. To optimize a component, an initial design is evolved based on a classical textbook approach coupled with general design expertise. The stress analyst will then define the elements that, from experience, he considers best fit the component. The resultant analysis will show areas of high or low stress or strain.

If it is considered that changes are necessary to the design, then the designer and stress analyst will decide the modifications to be made prior to carrying out a further analysis.

As programs become simpler to use, there is the temptation to design the ultimate component. When deciding whether a design is satisfactory, the limitations of the program, imposed by machining accuracies and material properties, and possible advantages, particularly in terms of mass, must all be taken into account. Programs for rocket motor design should be able to deal with almost any stress problem featuring almost any geometry, complete anisotropy, various material constitutive formulations, regions of differing materials in the same body, and thermal, pressure, acceleration, or point loadings in combination. Both twoand three-dimensional problems should be handled, and the analysis should not be limited to small strains or small deflections.

A major finite element program will offer about a hundred different element types ranging from those with very general applications, such as brick elements, to special elements exemplified by those that simulate a crack tip. The analysis tends to yield relatively inaccurate results if elements exhibit long, thin aspect ratios or corner angles near to zero or 180 deg. Usually these situations can be avoided, but difficulties can be experienced with long, thin adhesive layers in bonded structures.

The two elements most frequently used for the analysis of components are the quadrilateral, with its degenerate form the triangle, and the hexahedron, with its degenerate form the triangular prism or tetrahedron. The quadrilateral is used as a plane element in either plane strain or plane stress or as an axisymmetric element under axisymmetric loading. For many applications an adequate initial solution may be obtained by making an analysis using slight modifications to the geometry to bring it within the scope of this element. Applications include motor bodies, end rings, and reasonably axisymmetric end closures in the axisymmetric mode. The hexahedron is used as a solid brick element in a three-dimensional analysis. A particular application would be a closure for a multinozzle motor or with nonsymmetric features and launch hooks.

Idealization is the process of representing a real body or structure in terms of its geometry, material properties, loading, and constraint by a mathematical model that is amenable to analysis by the finite element method. Very often, an insignificant modification to geometry, loading, or support will result in a very great reduction in the computing and data preparation times. The term insignificant depends on behavior under load and also on which aspect of the result is under investigation. On the other hand, the model must possess adequate freedom or flexibility for the results to reflect the essential features of the real situation. The idealization process requires judgment and experience, and application of the finite element method is, therefore, not an exact science.

Finite element analysis should never be used uncritically or without insight. It is vital to ensure that the elements chosen do not lack a degree of freedom that must be taken into account and that they are sufficiently numerous for the model not to be significantly too stiff. Finite element models, in general, err on the side of being stiffer than the real item. Most finite element analyses used in rocket motor design are applied to a geometry of a type that has been analyzed previously and the expected behavior is known. In the case of a problem that is new to the user, it is good policy to do an initial run with a coarse division into elements to determine the general behavior.
